CAT-VARC-P06-001 · Evolution and life · passage Easy · 473 words · 4 questions

When a trait is described as highly heritable, many readers hear a claim about destiny: if height or some aspect of temperament is 'mostly genetic', then upbringing can do little to alter it. The inference feels natural, but it rests on a misunderstanding of what heritability measures. Heritability is a statistic about a population, not about a person. It estimates what share of the variation in a trait, across a particular group living in a particular range of environments, can be statistically associated with genetic differences among the members of that group. It says nothing about how much of any one individual's height was produced by genes, a question that is not even coherent, since every centimetre of a person's height required both genes and food.

Two consequences follow, and both tend to surprise people. First, heritability depends on the environment in which it is measured. If every child in a group were given exactly the same diet, schooling and care, the remaining differences among them would be largely genetic, and the heritability of almost any trait would rise, not because genes had become more powerful but because the environmental differences had been taken away. Conversely, in a group where some children are well fed and others go hungry, differences in nutrition will account for much of the variation in height, and the measured heritability will fall. Second, a trait can be built almost entirely by genes and still show low heritability. Nearly everyone is born with five fingers on each hand because a developmental programme laid down in the genome builds them; most people who have fewer have lost them through injury. Variation in finger number is therefore mainly environmental, even though the fingers themselves are anything but.

The most consequential error concerns differences between groups. Suppose a trait is highly heritable within each of two populations. It is tempting to conclude that the average difference between the populations must also be largely genetic. It need not be. Picture two handfuls of seed taken from the same genetically varied sack, one sown in rich soil and the other in poor soil. Within each plot, differences in height will be largely genetic, since the soil is uniform; between the plots, the difference is due entirely to the soil. High heritability within groups is compatible with a gap between them that has no genetic basis at all.

None of this makes heritability useless. Breeders rely on it to predict how quickly a herd will respond to selection, and researchers use it to judge where genetic studies are likely to find something. The fault lies not with the statistic but with the habit of reading it as a measure of fixity. A highly heritable trait may still be changed by changing the environment; short sight is substantially heritable, and spectacles correct it all the same.

CAT-VARC-P06-001-Q1 · Main idea / primary purpose · MediumMCQ
The primary purpose of the passage is to
  1. (A)argue that average differences between human populations are environmental rather than genetic.
  2. (B)correct a widespread habit of treating heritability as a measure of how unchangeable a trait is.
  3. (C)explain how breeders use heritability to predict how quickly a herd will respond to selection.
  4. (D)show that heritability estimates are too unreliable to be of use to breeders or to researchers.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 1 names the error of hearing heritability as 'a claim about destiny', paragraphs 2 and 3 show why the reading fails, and paragraph 4 concludes that 'The fault lies not with the statistic but with the habit of reading it as a measure of fixity'. The passage exists to correct that habit.

Why not (A): Paragraph 3 says only that a gap between groups 'need not be' genetic; the passage never asserts that actual human group differences are environmental, so this overstates and widens its claim.

Why not (C): Breeding is mentioned in a single clause of paragraph 4 as an example of proper use; it is a detail, not the purpose of the passage.

Why not (D): Paragraph 4 says 'None of this makes heritability useless' and gives breeders and researchers as legitimate users, so the option reverses the author's view.

Common misconception: Choosing the option about group differences, which magnifies one paragraph into a sweeping empirical claim.

Difficulty: One wrong option turns a careful 'need not be' into a positive claim; the correct answer needs the whole arc of the passage. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P06-001-Q2 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ
According to the passage, if every child in a group received exactly the same diet, schooling and care, the heritability of most traits would rise because
  1. (A)the environmental differences among the children would have been removed, leaving mainly genetic ones.
  2. (B)identical upbringing would allow the children's genes to exert a stronger influence on development.
  3. (C)children raised in identical conditions come to resemble their parents more closely in every trait.
  4. (D)measurements taken in uniform settings are more precise than measurements taken in varied ones.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 2 says heritability would rise 'because the environmental differences had been taken away', so the differences that remain are largely genetic.

Why not (B): Paragraph 2 says the rise would happen 'not because genes had become more powerful', which directly rejects this explanation.

Why not (C): The passage never discusses resemblance to parents; the explanation it gives is the removal of environmental differences.

Why not (D): Precision of measurement is not mentioned anywhere; the rise is explained by what varies in the group, not by how it is measured.

Common misconception: Picking the 'stronger genes' option, which the passage explicitly rules out.

Difficulty: The answer is stated in one sentence and the main wrong option is explicitly denied there. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P06-001-Q3 · Structure / function · EasyMCQ
The example of finger number in paragraph 2 is used to show that
  1. (A)accidents are the main source of variation in most human physical traits.
  2. (B)developmental programmes are more dependable than environmental influences on the body.
  3. (C)a trait built by a genetic programme can still show low heritability.
  4. (D)heritability rises whenever the environments of a group are made more uniform.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

The example follows 'Second, a trait can be built almost entirely by genes and still show low heritability': fingers are built by a genomic programme, yet the variation in their number is 'mainly environmental'.

Why not (A): The passage says injury explains variation in finger number only; it makes no claim about most human traits, so the option overgeneralises.

Why not (B): The passage compares no such dependability; the example concerns what heritability measures, not which influence is more reliable.

Why not (D): That is the first consequence in paragraph 2, illustrated by identical upbringing; the finger example introduces the 'Second' consequence, which is a different point.

Common misconception: Attaching the example to the first consequence in the same paragraph.

Difficulty: The sentence introducing the example states its function. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P06-001-Q4 · Application / analogy · EasyMCQ
A study finds that within each of two towns, differences in children's reading scores are highly heritable, and that the average score in one town is well above that in the other. Which conclusion is most consistent with the passage?
  1. (A)Reading scores in the lower town cannot be raised much by improving its schools or its libraries.
  2. (B)The gap between the towns must be largely genetic, since heritability is high within each town.
  3. (C)The study must be mistaken, since a trait cannot be heritable within towns that differ on average.
  4. (D)The gap between the towns could be produced entirely by differences between their schools or homes.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

The towns correspond to the two plots in paragraph 3: within each, variation can be largely genetic, while the difference between them may be due 'entirely to the soil', here the schools or homes.

Why not (A): Paragraph 4 says a highly heritable trait 'may still be changed by changing the environment', so reading heritability as fixity is the error the passage attacks.

Why not (B): This is exactly the inference paragraph 3 calls 'the most consequential error'; high heritability within groups is compatible with a gap that 'has no genetic basis at all'.

Why not (C): The seed example in paragraph 3 shows that high heritability within each plot and a large gap between plots can coexist, so nothing here is contradictory.

Common misconception: Treating within-group heritability as evidence about the cause of a between-group gap.

Difficulty: A direct transfer of the seed-plot analogy to a new case. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P06-002 · Evolution and life · passage Easy · 421 words · 4 questions

For anyone who thinks of natural selection as a relentless editor of waste, the tail of a peacock is an embarrassment, since it is heavy, conspicuous and awkward in flight, and it makes its bearer easier for predators to spot and harder for him to escape. Darwin treated such ornaments as a problem of their own, and proposed that they spread not because they helped their owners survive but because they helped them mate: females preferred them, and the preference was enough to carry the trait forward. That answer, however, raised a second and more awkward question, namely why females should come to prefer an ornament that makes their mates less likely to survive.

One influential reply turns the embarrassment into the explanation. An ornament can work as an honest signal precisely because it is costly. A male in poor condition cannot afford to grow and carry an extravagant tail; only a male with ample reserves, and perhaps with sound inherited qualities, can bear the burden and still thrive. A female who chooses the most lavish display is therefore choosing, without knowing it, the male best able to pay. On this view the cost is not a side effect to be minimised but the very thing that keeps the signal reliable. A cheap signal could be produced by anyone, and a signal that anyone can produce soon carries no information.

The idea has been extended well beyond courtship. Some antelope, on spotting a predator, leap high into the air on stiff legs instead of fleeing at once, which looks like a waste of precious seconds. If the leap advertises that the animal is fit enough to outrun any chase, a predator that heeds it saves itself a fruitless pursuit, and the leaper is spared an attack. Here too, the display persuades because it could not be produced by an animal that lacked the quality it advertises.

It would be a mistake, though, to treat costliness as the whole story. Theoretical work suggests that what matters is not that a signal is expensive in absolute terms but that it is more expensive for weak individuals than for strong ones; a signal equally costly for all would sort no one. Nor need every ornament be honest. Some preferences may persist simply because females who hold them have sons who inherit the admired trait, a self-reinforcing loop that can carry ornaments well beyond any useful information. The costly signal, in short, explains why many displays can be trusted, not why every display exists.

CAT-VARC-P06-002-Q1 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ
According to the passage, a signal that any animal can produce
  1. (A)is preferred by females because it costs its bearer very little to display.
  2. (B)soon stops telling the receiver anything about the animal that sends it.
  3. (C)is the kind most often found among antelope when they spot a predator.
  4. (D)is eventually replaced by an ornament that females come to find more beautiful.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 2 ends: 'A cheap signal could be produced by anyone, and a signal that anyone can produce soon carries no information.'

Why not (A): Paragraph 2 argues the reverse: the cost is 'the very thing that keeps the signal reliable', so cheapness is no ground for preference.

Why not (C): The antelope leap is offered in paragraph 3 as a display that 'could not be produced' by an unfit animal, the opposite of a signal anyone can produce.

Why not (D): The passage says nothing of cheap signals being replaced by more beautiful ones; it says only that they 'carry no information'.

Common misconception: Connecting the antelope example to cheap signals because both appear near the middle of the passage.

Difficulty: Located in a single sentence. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P06-002-Q2 · Meaning in context · EasyMCQ
In paragraph 2, 'the embarrassment' that the reply 'turns ... into the explanation' refers to
  1. (A)the burden that an extravagant ornament places on the animal that carries it.
  2. (B)Darwin's failure to propose any explanation at all for why such ornaments exist.
  3. (C)the awkwardness of the courtship display that the peacock performs before females.
  4. (D)the difficulty that females face in judging which of many males is healthiest.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 1 calls the peacock's tail 'an embarrassment' for a view of selection as an 'editor of waste', because it is heavy and dangerous. Paragraph 2 makes that very costliness the reason the signal is reliable.

Why not (B): Paragraph 1 says Darwin did propose an explanation, female preference; the embarrassment is the cost of the tail, not a gap in Darwin's work.

Why not (C): The passage describes the tail as 'awkward in flight', not the courtship as awkward; the embarrassment is the tail's cost to survival.

Why not (D): Female difficulty in judging males is never called an embarrassment; the word in paragraph 1 refers to the heavy, conspicuous tail.

Common misconception: Reading 'embarrassment' as a personal failure of Darwin's rather than as the costly trait.

Difficulty: The reference is fixed by the first sentence of paragraph 1. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P06-002-Q3 · Inference · MediumMCQ
The remark that 'a signal equally costly for all would sort no one' (paragraph 4) implies that
  1. (A)the more expensive a display is in absolute terms, the more reliably it identifies the fittest males.
  2. (B)most of the ornaments seen in nature cost every member of a species roughly the same amount.
  3. (C)females are unable to tell males apart once every male in a population has an ornament.
  4. (D)a display reveals quality only if weaker animals find it harder to bear than stronger ones do.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

The preceding clause says a signal works when it is 'more expensive for weak individuals than for strong ones'. If the cost fell equally, every animal could bear it to the same degree and the display would not distinguish strong from weak.

Why not (A): Paragraph 4 says what matters 'is not that a signal is expensive in absolute terms', so tying reliability to sheer expense reverses the point.

Why not (B): The passage makes no claim about how costs are distributed among most real ornaments; the remark is about what would follow if costs were equal.

Why not (C): The remark concerns signals of equal cost, not the mere presence of ornaments; males with ornaments may still differ in how well they bear them.

Common misconception: Choosing the 'more expensive is more reliable' option, which keeps the theme of cost but misses the point about unequal cost.

Difficulty: The tempting wrong option matches the general idea of paragraph 2 but contradicts the refinement in paragraph 4. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P06-002-Q4 · Author's tone / attitude · MediumMCQ
The author's attitude towards the costly-signal explanation is best described as
  1. (A)neutral, setting out the explanation without indicating any judgement of its merits.
  2. (B)sceptical, regarding it as an ingenious proposal that has gathered little real support.
  3. (C)appreciative of what it explains while insistent that it does not explain everything.
  4. (D)unreserved, treating it as a complete account of why ornaments evolve in animals.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

The author presents the idea favourably in paragraphs 2 and 3, then limits it in paragraph 4: it 'explains why many displays can be trusted, not why every display exists'.

Why not (A): The author judges the idea explicitly, both in its favour and in the qualification 'not why every display exists', so the stance is not neutral.

Why not (B): The author calls it 'influential', extends it to antelope and says it 'explains why many displays can be trusted'; nothing suggests it lacks support.

Why not (D): Paragraph 4 says it would be 'a mistake ... to treat costliness as the whole story' and gives a rival mechanism, so the author is not unreserved.

Common misconception: Picking 'unreserved' after reading only the middle paragraphs.

Difficulty: Requires weighing the favourable exposition against the closing qualification. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P06-003 · Evolution and life · passage Medium · 403 words · 4 questions

A familiar complaint holds that the theory of natural selection is empty at its core. Its slogan, survival of the fittest, seems to define the fittest as those who survive, so that the principle reduces to the statement that survivors survive. A claim that cannot be false, the complaint continues, explains nothing; it merely relabels what happened. The objection is old, and it has a certain surface appeal, but it depends on running together two different things: the record of which organisms in fact left offspring and the properties that made them likely to do so.

Consider two moths of the same species, one pale and one dark, resting on bark darkened by soot. The dark moth is fitter in that setting, but not because it happens to have left more offspring. It is fitter because its colouring makes it harder for birds to see, and that is a fact one can establish independently, by measuring how often birds find each kind of moth on each kind of bark. Fitness, properly understood, is a propensity: an expected rate of reproduction given an organism's traits and its surroundings. Like the fragility of a glass, it is a disposition that holds whether or not it is ever displayed. A fragile glass may survive a lifetime on a shelf, and a well-camouflaged moth may die in an unlucky accident; neither fact refutes the disposition.

Seen this way, the theory makes claims that could turn out false. It predicts that when the propensities of variants differ, and the differences are heritable, the frequencies of the variants will shift in a direction one can anticipate from an engineering or ecological analysis of the traits. Such predictions are tested constantly, and some fail, which is precisely what a tautology could never do. The distinction also explains why biologists speak of drift: in small populations the lucky can outbreed the fit, and the actual record of reproduction can depart from the expected one. If fitness simply meant survival, drift would be unintelligible, since there would be no gap between expectation and outcome for chance to fill.

The critics are not wholly wrong, however. Biologists often measure fitness by counting offspring, because propensities are hard to observe directly, and careless writing can slide from the measurement to the definition. The remedy is not to abandon the concept but to keep the evidence for a disposition distinct from the disposition itself.

CAT-VARC-P06-003-Q1 · Structure / function · EasyMCQ
The comparison with the fragility of a glass in paragraph 2 serves mainly to
  1. (A)suggest that camouflaged moths are, in practice, as easily destroyed as glassware on a shelf.
  2. (B)show that fitness, like fragility, can be established only after the organism has died or survived.
  3. (C)argue that chance matters more than inherited traits in deciding which organisms survive.
  4. (D)show that fitness can be a real property of an organism even when outcomes fail to display it.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 2 calls fitness 'a disposition that holds whether or not it is ever displayed'. The glass that never breaks and the moth that dies by accident both show that a single outcome does not refute the disposition.

Why not (A): This reads the analogy literally; the glass is compared to the moth only in that both have dispositions that may go undisplayed.

Why not (B): The passage says fitness can be established 'independently', by measuring predation, before any outcome; the option reverses the point of the analogy.

Why not (C): The passage allows that chance can override fitness in particular cases, but it never ranks chance above traits in general.

Common misconception: Taking the analogy literally or as a claim about the power of chance.

Difficulty: The function is stated in the sentence that introduces the comparison. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P06-003-Q2 · Inference · MediumMCQ
It can be inferred that the author regards the existence of drift as
  1. (A)evidence that fitness and the actual record of reproduction are not the same thing.
  2. (B)the main reason why biologists usually measure fitness by counting offspring.
  3. (C)a difficulty that the propensity view of fitness cannot yet satisfactorily explain.
  4. (D)a sign that natural selection does not operate at all in small populations.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 3 argues that drift would be 'unintelligible' if fitness meant survival, because there would be 'no gap between expectation and outcome for chance to fill'. Drift therefore shows the gap between fitness and the reproductive record.

Why not (B): Paragraph 4 says biologists count offspring 'because propensities are hard to observe directly'; drift is not given as the reason.

Why not (C): The author says the distinction 'explains why biologists speak of drift', so drift supports rather than troubles the propensity view.

Why not (D): Paragraph 3 says only that in small populations 'the lucky can outbreed the fit'; that chance can override selection does not mean selection ceases to operate.

Common misconception: Overextending 'the lucky can outbreed the fit' into the claim that selection is absent.

Difficulty: Requires connecting the drift sentence back to the definition of fitness in paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P06-003-Q3 · Strengthen / weaken / assumption · DifficultMCQ
Which of the following, if true, would most weaken the author's reply to the complaint that natural selection is a tautology?
  1. (A)Some predictions made by applying the theory of natural selection to particular populations have turned out to be false.
  2. (B)For most traits, the only available way to tell which variant raises expected reproduction is to see which variant has in fact left more offspring.
  3. (C)In some populations, the variants that ecological analysis rates fittest sometimes leave fewer offspring than other variants do.
  4. (D)Field biologists studying wild populations usually estimate the fitness of a variant by counting the offspring it leaves.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

The reply depends on fitness being identifiable 'independently', by analysing traits as in the moth example. If, for most traits, fitness could be identified only through which variant reproduced more, the propensity would collapse back into the record and the complaint would regain force.

Why not (A): Paragraph 3 cites failed predictions as proof that the theory is not a tautology, so this finding supports the author's reply.

Why not (C): The author expects this: paragraph 3 says 'the lucky can outbreed the fit' and treats such gaps as support for the propensity view, so it does not weaken the reply.

Why not (D): Paragraph 4 already concedes that biologists 'often measure fitness by counting offspring' and argues that this is evidence for a disposition, not its definition.

Common misconception: Picking a fact that sounds damaging (unfit outbreeding fit, offspring counting, failed predictions) without checking that the author has already absorbed it.

Difficulty: All three wrong options look like objections but are anticipated by the author; the correct answer targets the hidden assumption of independent identification. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P06-003-Q4 · Except / not supported · MediumMCQ
The passage supports each of the following statements EXCEPT:
  1. (A)Loose writing about fitness can blur the difference between measuring it and defining it.
  2. (B)Whether a variant is fitter can, at least in some cases, be established without counting its offspring.
  3. (C)Predictions derived from natural selection have so far been confirmed whenever they were tested.
  4. (D)In small populations, organisms that are less fit can leave more offspring than fitter ones.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 3 says predictions 'are tested constantly, and some fail', and treats those failures as the mark of a non-tautological theory. A claim that every prediction has been confirmed contradicts the passage.

Why not (A): Supported: paragraph 4 says 'careless writing can slide from the measurement to the definition'.

Why not (B): Supported: paragraph 2 says the dark moth's advantage can be established 'independently, by measuring how often birds find each kind of moth'.

Why not (D): Supported: paragraph 3 says 'in small populations the lucky can outbreed the fit'.

Common misconception: Assuming that an author defending a theory must claim its predictions always succeed.

Difficulty: Each supported statement paraphrases a different paragraph; the unsupported one reverses a short clause. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P06-004 · Evolution and life · passage Medium · 468 words · 4 questions

The sterile worker castes of ants, bees and wasps troubled Darwin as much as almost any feature of the living world. A creature that never reproduces cannot pass on whatever traits make it a good worker, so it was hard to see how natural selection could have refined the elaborate behaviour of workers over many generations. Darwin's tentative answer was that selection might act on the family rather than on the individual, much as a breeder who wants a good strain of cattle keeps the relatives of an animal whose worth became clear only after it was slaughtered.

The idea was given a precise form only in the twentieth century. A gene that disposes its bearer to help relatives can spread even if the bearer leaves no offspring of its own, provided the help raises the reproduction of relatives who are likely to carry copies of the same gene. What matters is the product of two quantities, the benefit to the relative and the probability that the relative shares the gene, weighed against the cost to the helper. Sterility, on this account, is not an anomaly to be explained away but an extreme point on a continuum of helping, reached when a helper can do more for its genes by raising siblings than by raising young of its own.

For a time, a further detail seemed to make the case almost too neat. In ants, bees and wasps, males develop from unfertilised eggs, and a peculiarity of inheritance follows: sisters who share both parents are, on average, more closely related to one another than a mother is to her daughters. If workers are more closely related to their sisters than to their own potential offspring, raising sisters is the better genetic bargain, and sterile castes should arise readily in exactly these insects. The argument was elegant, and it was widely taught. It has not survived well. Termites, whose inheritance has no such peculiarity, have sterile castes too; the queens of many ants and bees mate with several males, which dilutes the relatedness among their daughters; and the great majority of species with this peculiar inheritance are not social at all.

The lesson is not that kinship is irrelevant. Workers in social insect colonies typically do raise relatives, and the general principle remains the best account of why they can afford to forgo reproduction. The lesson is rather that relatedness is only one term in the calculation. Ecological conditions that make independent breeding hopeless, such as a scarce nest site that must be defended or food that only a group can gather, raise the benefit of helping and can tip the balance even where relatedness is modest. The special feature of inheritance made a good story, but the more durable explanation lies in the interaction of kinship with circumstance.

CAT-VARC-P06-004-Q1 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ
Which of the following does the passage cite as evidence against the argument based on the peculiar inheritance of ants, bees and wasps?
  1. (A)A worker that never reproduces cannot pass on the traits that make it a good worker.
  2. (B)Males in ants, bees and wasps develop from eggs that have not been fertilised.
  3. (C)Termites, which lack that pattern of inheritance, have also evolved sterile castes.
  4. (D)Workers in social insect colonies typically raise relatives rather than unrelated young.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 3 lists three problems for the argument, the first being that 'Termites, whose inheritance has no such peculiarity, have sterile castes too'.

Why not (A): This is the puzzle in paragraph 1 that troubled Darwin; it is not one of the objections listed against the later argument.

Why not (B): This is the starting point of the argument in paragraph 3, so it cannot be evidence against it.

Why not (D): Paragraph 4 offers this in support of the general kinship principle, not as evidence against the argument from inheritance.

Common misconception: Picking a statement that appears in the passage but plays a different role.

Difficulty: The objections are listed in one sentence. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P06-004-Q2 · Inference · MediumMCQ
In describing sterility as 'an extreme point on a continuum of helping' (paragraph 2), the author implies that
  1. (A)sterility in insects evolved earlier than the milder forms of helping seen among relatives.
  2. (B)helping relatives is common only in those insect species whose workers are sterile.
  3. (C)workers lose the ability to reproduce as a consequence of the effort spent raising siblings.
  4. (D)sterile workers differ from other helpful relatives in how far they help, not in the kind of explanation they need.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 2 says sterility 'is not an anomaly to be explained away' but lies on a continuum, so the same calculation of benefit, relatedness and cost that explains ordinary helping explains sterility, which is simply its limit.

Why not (A): A continuum concerns degree, not chronology; the passage says nothing about which form of helping appeared first.

Why not (B): The phrase places sterility at one end of a range of helping, which implies that milder helping exists elsewhere; the option restricts helping to sterile species.

Why not (C): The passage treats sterility as a strategy favoured when helping pays more, not as damage caused by the work of helping, so the option reverses the relation.

Common misconception: Reading 'continuum' as a sequence in time, or reversing cause and effect.

Difficulty: Requires reading the metaphor against the preceding 'not an anomaly' clause. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P06-004-Q3 · Structure / function · MediumMCQ
Paragraph 3 functions in the passage mainly to
  1. (A)present an appealing refinement of the kinship argument and then show why it proved inadequate.
  2. (B)show that biologists have since abandoned the general kinship argument set out in paragraph 2.
  3. (C)explain why Darwin's proposal that selection acts on the family was eventually rejected.
  4. (D)supply the decisive evidence that ecological conditions, not kinship, explain sterile castes.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 3 introduces the 'almost too neat' argument from inheritance, notes it 'was widely taught', and then says 'It has not survived well', giving three objections.

Why not (B): Paragraph 4 says 'The lesson is not that kinship is irrelevant' and that the principle 'remains the best account', so the general argument survives.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 criticises the argument from peculiar inheritance, a later refinement; Darwin's family idea is developed in paragraph 2, not rejected.

Why not (D): Ecological conditions are introduced only in paragraph 4, and there they are combined with kinship rather than set against it.

Common misconception: Confusing the rejection of the refinement with rejection of kinship explanations as a whole.

Difficulty: The tempting wrong option overstates the rejection to cover paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P06-004-Q4 · Inference · DifficultMCQ
The author would most likely agree with which of the following?
  1. (A)Kinship plays only a minor part in explaining why workers in insect colonies forgo reproduction.
  2. (B)Two insect species with similar relatedness among nestmates might differ in whether sterile castes evolve, because of differences in their surroundings.
  3. (C)Sterile castes are unlikely to evolve in any species whose nestmates are only modestly related to one another.
  4. (D)Where a queen mates with several males, the colony cannot sustain a caste of sterile workers.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 4 makes relatedness 'only one term in the calculation' and says ecological conditions raise the benefit of helping. Holding relatedness equal, different conditions could therefore lead to different outcomes.

Why not (A): Paragraph 4 says the general kinship principle 'remains the best account' of why workers can forgo reproduction, so this understates its role.

Why not (C): Paragraph 4 says ecological conditions 'can tip the balance even where relatedness is modest', so the author would reject this.

Why not (D): Paragraph 3 cites multiple mating only as diluting relatedness and weakening one argument; it never says sterile castes then become impossible.

Common misconception: Treating the dilution of relatedness as fatal to sterile castes, which keeps the rejected argument's logic.

Difficulty: Each wrong option echoes a real sentence but shifts its strength; the correct answer must be derived from the final paragraph's two-factor view. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P06-005 · Evolution and life · passage Medium · 468 words · 4 questions

Until late in the twentieth century, many biologists expected that animals as different as a fly and a mouse would be built by largely different sets of genes. Their bodies seemed to have little in common: one has a hard external skeleton, six jointed legs and compound eyes, the other an internal skeleton, four limbs and eyes with a single lens. If form reflects the genes that make it, the reasoning went, such different forms must be made by different genes. What came instead was one of the great surprises of modern biology. A small kit of genes that direct development, deciding where the head goes, where limbs sprout and which segments become which, turned out to be shared, in recognisable versions, across almost the entire animal kingdom.

The most striking demonstrations concerned eyes. Eyes had long been thought to have evolved independently many times, so different are the eyes of insects, squid and vertebrates. Yet a gene needed for eye development in mice has a counterpart in flies, and when the mouse version is switched on in the wrong tissue of a developing fly, the fly grows extra eyes, fly eyes, on its legs or wings. The gene does not carry a blueprint of an eye. It acts more like a switch that says, in effect, 'build an eye here', leaving the details of construction to the many other genes that each lineage has in its own repertoire.

It is tempting to conclude that the evolution of form is mostly a matter of rewiring old switches rather than inventing new parts, and there is much truth in that. Changes in when and where toolkit genes are turned on can alter the number of segments, the length of a limb or the pattern on a wing without any change in the proteins the genes make. Because a single toolkit gene acts in many tissues, a change to the gene itself would tend to disrupt many structures at once, whereas a change to one of its switches can alter a single structure and leave the rest alone. This helps explain why so much evolutionary change appears to lie in the regulatory regions of the genome.

Yet the shared toolkit should not be mistaken for a shared plan. That the same switch triggers eye-building in a fly and a mouse does not mean that their eyes are, in any useful sense, the same organ; the structures downstream of the switch were assembled separately, over hundreds of millions of years, from different materials. Common genes reveal a deep common ancestry of the control system, not a common design for what is controlled. The older view was wrong to assume that different forms require different genes; the newer enthusiasm risks the opposite error, of assuming that similar genes imply similar forms.

  4. (D)a switch can alter a single structure while leaving the others in which the gene acts untouched.

Difficulty: The reason is given in one sentence. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P06-006 · Evolution and life · passage Medium · 479 words · 4 questions

We tend to read the history of life as a long tournament in which the better designed win. On this view a lineage that disappeared must have had some flaw, and the dinosaurs, the textbook losers, are pictured as sluggish and outmoded long before their end. The picture is comfortable because it makes the present inhabitants of the earth look like deserving champions. For the most dramatic episodes in the record it is also misleading. During ordinary stretches of geological time, extinction does seem to track the qualities that adaptation produces: species with narrow diets, small ranges or slow breeding are more likely to vanish when conditions shift, and their places are taken by competitors that cope better. The great mass extinctions, in which a large fraction of species disappeared within a geologically brief interval, seem to follow different rules.

Consider the end of the Cretaceous period, when an asteroid struck the earth and the non-avian dinosaurs vanished along with many other groups. What determined survival in the aftermath was not, as far as can be told, excellence at the ways of life that had served well for millions of years. It was the possession of traits that happened to matter in a sudden catastrophe: small body size, a varied diet, the ability to shelter or to wait out a period of darkness and scarcity in a dormant state. Such traits had not been selected for their value in a catastrophe of this kind, which no living lineage had experienced. They were, from the point of view of the crisis, accidents.

The consequence is that mass extinctions can redirect the history of life in ways that ordinary selection would not. A group that had been flourishing, and that would probably have continued to flourish under normal conditions, can be swept away, while a group that had been marginal for tens of millions of years inherits an emptied world. The mammals, which had lived in the shadow of the dinosaurs for a very long time, are the familiar example. Their later success says little about whether they were superior to the non-avian dinosaurs in any absolute sense; it says that they passed through a narrow gate that the dinosaurs, for reasons unrelated to their earlier success, did not.

This does not mean that mass extinctions are pure lotteries. Survival in them is selective; it simply selects for different things. Nor does it mean that adaptation is unimportant, since the survivors went on to diversify through ordinary selection once the crisis had passed. What it does mean is that the long-run shape of life's history cannot be read as a straightforward measure of design quality. To understand why some groups are here and others are not, we need to know not only how well they fitted their world but also which rare disasters that world happened to contain.

CAT-VARC-P06-007 · Evolution and life · passage Difficult · 633 words · 5 questions

Debates about the origin of life have long been organised around a chicken-and-egg problem. In every living cell today, the information needed to build proteins is stored in nucleic acids, while the work of copying those nucleic acids is done by proteins. Neither seems able to arise without the other. One family of proposals breaks the circle by giving priority to the molecule that carries information: life began, on this view, when some molecule capable of copying itself appeared, however clumsily, and natural selection among its variants did the rest. A rival family gives priority to chemistry that sustains itself: life began as networks of reactions, perhaps on mineral surfaces near hot springs on the sea floor, that captured energy from their surroundings and kept themselves going long before any of their components stored heritable information.

The case for putting the replicator first gained great force from a discovery about RNA, a close chemical relative of DNA. RNA, it turned out, can not only carry information but also catalyse reactions, and the core of the machinery that assembles proteins in every cell is itself made of RNA. A world in which RNA did both jobs, storing instructions and doing chemical work, would dissolve the chicken-and-egg problem, and that protein-building machinery looks very much like a relic of such a world. Replicator theorists add a deeper argument. Without heredity, they say, there is no evolution in any serious sense: a chemical network that merely persists cannot accumulate improvements, because there is nothing to record which variants did better.

Metabolism-first theorists reply that the RNA world has a chemistry problem it has never solved. The building blocks of RNA are intricate molecules, hard to make without the help of enzymes and prone to fall apart in water; assembling them into long chains that can copy themselves requires a degree of order that seems unlikely to arise unaided. Self-sustaining reaction networks, by contrast, need only simple ingredients and a source of energy. These theorists also argue that heredity need not wait for a dedicated molecule. A network in which certain reactions reinforce one another could persist in several alternative states, and if fragments that break away from a network tend to restart the same state, something like inheritance exists before any gene.

It is worth noticing that the two camps are often answering different questions. Replicator theorists ask what made open-ended evolution possible, and their answer, a molecule that copies information with modest accuracy, is hard to dispute as an account of that threshold. Metabolism theorists ask where the materials and energy for any such molecule came from, and their answer, a geochemical setting that drives reactions without the help of life, is equally hard to dispute as an account of the prior stage. Presented as rivals, each side scores easy points against the other's weakest claim: that replicators could assemble themselves in a chemical vacuum, or that self-maintaining networks could evolve indefinitely without anything like genes.

The more promising view, then, treats the problem as one of sequence rather than of priority in the abstract. A chemistry able to sustain itself may have been the setting in which the first replicators formed and on which they depended, and the replicators may in turn have captured and refined the network that fed them. This does not settle the matter, since the details of each stage remain obscure and may never be recovered from a planet whose earliest rocks have been largely destroyed. But it changes what counts as progress. The question is no longer which came first, as though one of them had to be the true beginning, but how a world of self-maintaining chemistry could have given rise to one in which information was inherited.

Why not (A): The passage says information is stored in nucleic acids and calls RNA a relative of DNA; it does not say RNA stores most instructions, and that is not the discovery reported.

Why not (B): No such ability is reported; paragraph 3 says RNA building blocks are 'prone to fall apart in water', and paragraph 4 speaks only of 'modest accuracy'.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 says the reverse: RNA's building blocks are 'hard to make without the help of enzymes'; simple ingredients describe reaction networks.

Common misconception: Choosing an option that attributes the metabolism camp's ingredients to RNA.

Difficulty: Stated in one sentence of paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P06-008 · Evolution and life · passage Difficult · 581 words · 5 questions

Suppose the history of life could be rewound to some early point and allowed to run again. Would anything like the present living world appear a second time? One school answers no. Evolution, on this view, is a chain of contingencies, each step dependent on the particular steps before it and on unrepeatable accidents of climate, geology and mutation. Alter any early link, and the chain would run off in another direction entirely; the existence of vertebrates, of flowering plants or of our own species would be no more to be expected than a particular sequence of cards in a shuffled deck. On this view the tree of life is a record of accidents, intelligible after the fact but not predictable before it, and any search for a direction in it reads our own presence back into a history that never had us in mind. The rival school points to the remarkable frequency with which evolution arrives at the same solution independently. Camera-like eyes have arisen separately in vertebrates and in octopuses; streamlined bodies have evolved in fish, in extinct marine reptiles and in dolphins; flight has been achieved by insects, pterosaurs, birds and bats. If the same designs keep recurring, the argument runs, the outcome of a second run would be broadly familiar.

Each school has a strong case, and each tends to treat the other's evidence as anecdote. Convergence enthusiasts can list hundreds of cases, but the list is assembled after the fact from the solutions that did recur, and it says nothing about the far larger number of forms that appeared once and never again. Contingency enthusiasts can point to those unrepeated forms, but they often treat the mere fact that a lineage had a particular history as proof that its outcome depended on that history, which does not follow: a ball may take a unique path down a hillside and still come to rest in the only hollow at the bottom.

Much of the disagreement, I want to suggest, dissolves once we notice that the two schools are describing life at different levels of detail. At the level of broad functional solutions, a second run would very probably produce organisms that see, swim, fly and filter food, because the physical problems of moving through water or air, or of forming an image from light, admit only a few good solutions. At the level of particular lineages, the replay would almost certainly differ: there is no reason to expect that the flying creatures would be birds, or that the swimmers would have backbones. Convergence is a claim about the space of workable designs; contingency is a claim about which occupants of that space history happens to supply. The two are compatible, and both are probably true.

This reconciliation has a cost, and it is worth stating plainly. If the question that matters is whether a replay would produce something like us, a self-aware animal that makes tools and uses language, then neither school's best evidence is decisive. Intelligence of our kind has arisen, so far as we know, only once, and a single case cannot reveal whether it was a likely destination or a fluke. The recurrence of eyes and wings tells us that some problems have few solutions; it does not tell us that every capacity we value is among those solutions. On the questions that most excite the public, the honest answer is that the evidence does not yet discriminate between the two views.

CAT-VARC-P06-008-Q1 · Inference · MediumMCQ
The image of the ball in paragraph 2 implies that
  1. (A)the outcomes of evolution are fixed in advance by the physical shape of the environment.
  2. (B)the history of a lineage is of no use in understanding what that lineage became.
  3. (C)an end point reached by a route no one could repeat may still have been very likely to be reached.
  4. (D)contingency enthusiasts are wrong to claim that many forms appeared only once.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

The image follows the charge that contingency enthusiasts treat a particular history as 'proof that its outcome depended on that history, which does not follow'. A ball's unique path can still end in 'the only hollow', so a unique route is compatible with an all but inevitable result.

Why not (A): The image shows only that a unique history does not prove a contingent outcome; it does not claim that all outcomes are fixed in advance.

Why not (B): The point is that history alone does not prove dependence, not that history is useless; the author never dismisses lineage histories.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 accepts that a 'far larger number of forms' appeared once; the image targets a different inference made by the contingency school.

Common misconception: Overextending the image into a claim that outcomes are predetermined.

Difficulty: The image must be read against the specific inference it rebuts. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P06-008-Q2 · Author's tone / attitude · MediumMCQ
The author's attitude towards the two schools is best described as
  1. (A)partisan, favouring the convergence school and treating the contingency school's case as anecdote.
  2. (B)even-handed, crediting each with a real insight while faulting how it treats evidence.
  3. (C)dismissive, regarding the entire debate between them as confused and not worth pursuing.
  4. (D)deferential, accepting the claims of each school and declining to judge between them.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 2 opens 'Each school has a strong case' and then criticises the convergence list as 'assembled after the fact' and the contingency inference as one that 'does not follow'. Paragraph 3 holds that both claims are compatible and 'probably true'.

Why not (C): The author says only that 'much of the disagreement' dissolves, and treats the remaining question about intelligence as open and important.

CAT-VARC-P06-008-Q3 · Strengthen / weaken / assumption · DifficultMCQ
Which of the following, if true, would most weaken the reconciliation proposed in paragraph 3?
  1. (A)Intelligence that is capable of language and the making of tools has arisen in only one lineage of animals so far.
  2. (B)Far more forms of life have appeared once in the history of life and never recurred than have appeared repeatedly.
  3. (C)A replay of evolution would probably not produce birds as the main flying animals, nor backboned animals as the main swimmers.
  4. (D)Most supposed independent origins of eyes, wings and streamlined bodies turn out to descend from one shared ancestral form.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

The functional-level half of the reconciliation, that a replay would 'very probably' produce seeing, swimming and flying organisms, rests on the recurrence of these designs as evidence that physical problems 'admit only a few good solutions'. If the recurrences were really inherited from a single origin, that evidence would vanish.

Why not (A): Paragraph 4 states this as a limit on what the evidence shows; it bears on the separate question of intelligence, not on the two-level reconciliation.

Why not (B): Paragraph 2 already grants this, and paragraph 3 assigns such unrepeated forms to the level of particular lineages, so it is consistent with the reconciliation.

Why not (C): This is what paragraph 3 itself predicts at 'the level of particular lineages', so it supports rather than weakens the reconciliation.

Common misconception: Choosing the unrepeated-forms option because it sounds like evidence for contingency against convergence.

Difficulty: All three wrong options are statements the passage itself makes or accepts; the correct answer targets the evidence on which one level of the reconciliation depends. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P06-008-Q4 · Inference · DifficultMCQ
It can be inferred from paragraph 4 that the author regards the repeated evolution of eyes and wings as
  1. (A)good evidence that intelligence like ours was most probably a fluke of one lineage's history.
  2. (B)good evidence that intelligence like ours is a likely outcome of any rerun of evolution.
  3. (C)irrelevant to any question about what a replay of evolution would be likely to produce.
  4. (D)insufficient grounds for expecting a replay of evolution to produce a creature like us.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 4 says the recurrence shows only that 'some problems have few solutions' and that on intelligence the evidence 'does not yet discriminate between the two views'. It is relevant to broad functions but not enough to predict a creature like us.

Why not (A): The author says 'a single case cannot reveal whether it was a likely destination or a fluke', so the author does not take the evidence to favour the fluke reading either.

Why not (B): Paragraph 4 says the recurrence of eyes and wings 'does not tell us that every capacity we value is among those solutions', which denies this.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 uses that recurrence to predict that a replay would 'very probably' produce seeing and flying organisms, so it is relevant to some questions.

Common misconception: Choosing the 'fluke' option, which wrongly takes the author's scepticism about convergence as support for contingency.

CAT-VARC-P06-008-Q5 · Application / analogy · DifficultMCQ
Which of the following situations is most analogous to the reconciliation of the two schools in paragraph 3?
  1. (A)A town rebuilt after a flood would almost surely again have bakeries and schools, though chance would decide who ran them.
  2. (B)A river fed by the same snowmelt every spring rises on the same day each year and always reaches exactly the same height.
  3. (C)A chess game replayed from the same opening position follows a different course every time and ends in a different result.
  4. (D)Two engineers working apart design nearly identical bridges because both have copied the same famous earlier bridge.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 3 says broad functional roles recur because problems 'admit only a few good solutions', while 'which occupants of that space history happens to supply' is contingent. Bakeries and schools are the recurring roles; who runs them is the contingent occupancy.

Why not (B): Nothing in this case varies from year to year, so it shows only a fixed, recurring outcome; it lacks the second level at which chance decides the particular occupants of a recurring role.

Why not (C): This captures only contingency, with no level at which the replays reliably converge on the same broad outcome.

Why not (D): The similarity here comes from a shared source, which is not convergence on a solution arrived at independently.

Common misconception: Picking the river, which illustrates convergence alone, or the bridges, which mistake copying for convergence.

Difficulty: The analogy must reproduce both levels of the reconciliation, and each wrong option reproduces at most one. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P06-009 · Ecology and conservation · passage Easy · 426 words · 4 questions

Few environmental gestures are as photogenic as planting a tree. A minister kneels with a spade, a schoolchild pats soil around a sapling, and a campaign announces that so many million trees have gone into the ground this season. The appeal is easy to understand. A tree is a visible, countable object, and counting is what campaigns need: a target can be set, met and reported. Yet the habit of measuring forest recovery by the number of saplings planted has quietly confused an input with an outcome, and the confusion has consequences for the land it is meant to help.

The first problem is survival. A sapling planted in the wrong season, in compacted soil or without anyone to water it through its first dry months often dies within a year or two, and a campaign that counts plantings rather than living trees will never record the loss. The second problem is choice. Large drives tend to favour a few fast-growing species that nurseries can raise cheaply and in bulk, and the result, where the saplings do survive, may be closer to a plantation than a forest: rows of a single kind of tree, offering little to the insects, birds and fungi that a mixed woodland supports. In some places such planting has even been carried out on grasslands that were never forested at all, replacing one valuable habitat with a poor imitation of another.

An alternative, less dramatic and much harder to photograph, is to let forests come back on their own. On land where old roots, nearby seed-bearing trees and a living soil remain, simply protecting an area from grazing, fire and clearing can allow a varied forest to regenerate, often more cheaply than planting and with species suited to the site because they arrived there by themselves. Regeneration of this kind is slow at first, and for some years the land looks like scrub, which is one reason it is undervalued: it does not look like progress.

None of this means that planting is always a mistake. Where soils are badly degraded, where no seed sources survive nearby, or where a particular species must be reintroduced, careful planting may be the only way to begin. The objection is not to the spade but to the scoreboard. A campaign that measured success by the area of land that becomes, and remains, a functioning forest after ten or twenty years would sometimes plant and sometimes refrain, and it would be judged by what grew rather than by what was put into the ground.

CAT-VARC-P06-009-Q1 · Main idea / primary purpose · EasyMCQ
The primary purpose of the passage is to
  1. (A)criticise public figures who take part in planting ceremonies mainly in order to be photographed doing something popular.
  2. (B)argue that forest recovery should be judged by the lasting forest it produces rather than by the number of saplings that are planted.
  3. (C)argue that tree planting campaigns should be replaced everywhere by the protection of land, so that forests can regenerate naturally without any planting.
  4. (D)explain the practical techniques by which nurseries and planters can raise the survival rate of the saplings they put into the ground.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 1 states the thesis: counting saplings 'has quietly confused an input with an outcome'. Paragraphs 2 and 3 show the costs of that confusion and an alternative, and paragraph 4 concludes that success should be measured by land that 'becomes, and remains, a functioning forest'.

Why not (A): The minister with a spade in paragraph 1 illustrates why planting appeals to campaigns; the passage does not attack anyone's motives, and its target is the measure of success.

Why not (C): Paragraph 4 says 'None of this means that planting is always a mistake' and names cases where planting 'may be the only way to begin', so a blanket replacement contradicts the author.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 mentions why saplings die, but only as evidence that counting plantings hides losses; the passage offers no techniques for improving survival.

Common misconception: Overstating the argument into a rejection of all planting, which the last paragraph explicitly rules out.

Difficulty: The thesis is stated in paragraph 1 and restated in paragraph 4; wrong options are clearly too broad or too narrow. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P06-009-Q4 · Application / analogy · MediumMCQ
Which of the following programmes would the author of the passage most likely regard as best designed?

  2. (B)One that refuses to plant anywhere, including on badly degraded soils, and instead fences every site and waits for a forest to return on its own.
  3. (C)One that fences off land near surviving seed trees, plants only on degraded plots with no seed source, and reports how much forest still stands after fifteen years.

Why not (B): Paragraph 4 says that where soils are badly degraded or no seed sources survive, 'careful planting may be the only way to begin', so refusing to plant anywhere goes against the author.

CAT-VARC-P06-010 · Ecology and conservation · passage Easy · 432 words · 4 questions

Conservation has always had favourites. Appeals for money are illustrated with tigers, pandas, whales and elephants, animals with large eyes, recognisable faces and a reputation for grandeur, and rarely with a beetle, a freshwater mussel or a lichen. Critics have long complained that this distorts priorities. If donors give to what they find beautiful, the argument runs, then most of the living world, which is small, drab or simply invisible, will be neglected, and the species most in need of help will be the least likely to receive it. The objection is not merely aesthetic. Insects, fungi and the small organisms of soil and water do much of the work that keeps ecosystems running, yet they attract only a tiny share of public concern.

The complaint has force, but it is weaker than it first appears, because a charismatic animal rarely receives protection on its own. A large predator needs a large area, and the forest or grassland that is protected for its sake also shelters thousands of less appealing species that no donor would have paid to save directly. In this sense the famous animal works as a kind of advertisement for an entire landscape. The money is raised in the name of one face, but it is spent, at least in part, on soil, water and cover that serve everything living there.

The defence holds only up to a point, however. Some of the most threatened organisms live in habitats that no charismatic animal shares: a single cave, a spring, a patch of chalk grassland, the gravel bed of one river. Protecting a tiger reserve will do nothing for them. Moreover, money given in the name of a famous animal is sometimes spent on that animal directly, on breeding programmes, tracking collars or patrols against poachers, rather than on the wider habitat, and in such cases the advertising argument does not apply at all. A donor who pays for a radio collar has helped one tiger, and perhaps the scientists who follow it, but not the river below the reserve.

The sensible conclusion is neither to abandon flagship species nor to treat them as a complete strategy. They are a tool for raising attention and money, and a useful one. But a conservation movement that allowed the tastes of donors to draw its map of priorities would leave large gaps, and those gaps would fall precisely where the most unfamiliar forms of life are found. Some part of the effort has to be directed by need rather than by appeal, even when need comes in the shape of a mussel.

  3. (C)breeding programmes and tracking collars for the predator also yield information about the other species.
  4. (D)the predator keeps down the animals that would otherwise eat or crowd out the less appealing species.

Paragraph 2: 'A large predator needs a large area, and the forest or grassland that is protected for its sake also shelters thousands of less appealing species'.

Why not (B): Paragraph 2 says these species are ones 'no donor would have paid to save directly'; the benefit comes through shared habitat, not through donors changing their giving.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 mentions breeding programmes and collars as spending on the animal itself, precisely the case in which 'the advertising argument does not apply'.

Why not (D): The passage never describes the predator's effect on other animals; its point concerns the area protected 'for its sake'.

Common misconception: Supplying an ecological mechanism (predation) that the passage does not mention.

Difficulty: Stated directly in paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P06-011 · Ecology and conservation · passage Medium · 406 words · 4 questions

When a population of elephants recovers, the news is usually reported as a conservation triumph, and in an important sense it is one. But the recovery is experienced very differently at the edge of the forest. For a farming household whose maize or paddy lies along an elephant's route, a single night's visit can destroy a season's income, and the danger is not only to crops: people walking home after dark, or guarding fields from makeshift platforms, are injured and sometimes killed. The benefits of a thriving elephant population are spread thinly across a nation, or across the world; its costs are concentrated on a few villages.

Discussions of the problem often frame it as one of attitudes. Farmers who retaliate, it is said, need to be taught to value wildlife, and coexistence is presented as a disposition that can be cultivated through awareness campaigns. There is something to this, since tolerance of wild animals varies a great deal between communities and is shaped by tradition as well as by loss. Yet to treat hostility as ignorance is to misread it. A farmer who has lost three harvests to elephants may understand their ecological importance perfectly well and still be unable to afford them.

The more honest framing is distributive. Coexistence is not a state of mind but an arrangement about who bears which costs. Compensation schemes acknowledge this in principle, but in practice they are often slow, demand paperwork that poor households struggle to complete, and pay for the market value of a damaged crop while ignoring the sleepless nights, the children kept from school to guard fields and the fear that hangs over every harvest. A scheme that pays a fraction of the loss, a year late, does not shift the burden; it documents it.

Practical deterrents have their place. Some communities have experimented with fences hung with beehives, which elephants tend to avoid, or with repellents made from chilli and with networks that warn villages of approaching herds, and where these work they reduce losses at modest cost. But deterrents move the problem as often as they solve it, pushing elephants onto the next village's fields. The underlying question remains political: whether the wider society that values elephants is willing to pay, promptly and in full, those who live beside them. Until it is, appeals for tolerance ask the poorest people in the landscape to subsidise everyone else's enjoyment of a recovered species.

  3. (C)pay farmers more than their crops were worth, which removes any reason for them to guard their fields at night.
  4. (D)reach only those farmers who have retaliated against elephants and so reward hostility rather than tolerance.

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 3: schemes 'pay for the market value of a damaged crop while ignoring the sleepless nights, the children kept from school to guard fields and the fear that hangs over every harvest'.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 says schemes pay 'a fraction of the loss', the reverse of overpayment, and never suggests they discourage guarding.

Common misconception: Inventing a reason (who runs the schemes) that sounds plausible but is not given.

Difficulty: Located in one sentence of paragraph 3. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P06-011-Q3 · Strengthen / weaken / assumption · MediumMCQ

CAT-VARC-P06-011-Q4 · Inference · DifficultMCQ
The author's remark that deterrents push 'elephants onto the next village's fields' suggests that
  1. (A)neighbouring villages ought to coordinate their deterrents so that elephants are steered back into the forest.
  2. (B)a remedy that lowers one village's losses may only pass them to neighbours, leaving the wider question of who pays unresolved.
  3. (C)the costs of a recovered elephant population are in fact spread evenly across the villages that border the forest.
  4. (D)beehive fences and chilli repellents seldom reduce losses at all, since elephants soon learn to ignore them and return to the same fields.

Paragraph 4 grants that deterrents work locally but says they 'move the problem as often as they solve it'. The next sentence states that 'The underlying question remains political', so the remark shows that technical fixes redistribute costs among those already bearing them instead of transferring them to the wider society.

Why not (A): The author proposes no coordination; the conclusion turns instead to whether 'the wider society' will pay those who live beside elephants.

Why not (C): Moving damage from one village to the next does not make costs even, and paragraph 1 says they are 'concentrated on a few villages'.

Why not (D): Paragraph 4 says that 'where these work they reduce losses at modest cost'; the remark is about losses moving, not about deterrents failing or elephants learning.

Common misconception: Reading the remark as saying deterrents do not work, when the passage says they do work locally.

Difficulty: Requires linking the remark to the distributive thesis and separating 'move the problem' from 'fail'. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P06-012 · Ecology and conservation · passage Medium · 423 words · 4 questions

For much of the twentieth century, forest agencies in several countries treated fire as an enemy to be defeated wherever it appeared. The logic seemed unanswerable: fire destroys timber, threatens homes and kills animals, so every fire extinguished was a loss averted. Agencies measured their performance by how quickly they put fires out and how few hectares burned, and by those measures many of them succeeded remarkably well. Lookout towers, firebreaks and crews ready to move at short notice made it possible to reach most fires while they were still small.

The success contained its own reversal. In many dry forests, frequent fires of low intensity had once cleared the undergrowth, fallen branches and young trees that gather on the forest floor, while leaving the older, thick-barked trees largely unharmed. When such fires were suppressed year after year, that material was left to build up. The forest grew denser, and the fuel that would once have burned off in small, manageable fires accumulated until, in a hot, dry season, a fire that could not be stopped found enough of it to climb into the crowns of the trees. Such fires kill the very trees that the earlier, smaller ones had spared. By preventing many small fires, suppression had, in effect, been saving up for a few enormous ones.

The lesson is sometimes drawn too broadly. Not every ecosystem evolved with frequent fire; in some wet forests and many shrublands fire was historically rare, and a recent increase in burning there is a threat rather than a return to normal. Nor does the history of suppression explain every catastrophic fire, since drought, heat and the spread of housing into wooded land matter as well. The argument is not that fire is good, but that whether a given fire is harmful depends on the ecosystem in which it burns and on that ecosystem's history.

Recognising this has not made the remedy easy. Deliberately setting fires under controlled conditions, or allowing some natural fires to burn, can reduce accumulated fuel, but it carries risks that suppression appeared not to. Politically, a season without burning looks like competence. Smoke drifts over towns, and a planned burn that escapes is blamed on the people who lit it, while a wildfire that grows from decades of accumulated fuel is blamed on nature. A manager therefore faces an asymmetry. The harm of acting is visible and attributable; the harm of not acting is deferred and diffuse, and the easiest course is to keep doing what made the problem worse.

  2. (B)were started deliberately by forest agencies to reduce the fuel that had built up under years of suppression.
  3. (C)killed off the older trees so that young trees and undergrowth could grow in the light that was let in.
  4. (D)climbed into the crowns of the trees but burned out before they could spread to neighbouring stands.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 2: frequent low-intensity fires 'cleared the undergrowth, fallen branches and young trees that gather on the forest floor, while leaving the older, thick-barked trees largely unharmed'.

Why not (B): The low-intensity fires described in paragraph 2 came before suppression; deliberate burning appears only in paragraph 4 as a modern remedy.

Why not (C): Paragraph 2 says the opposite: these fires cleared 'young trees' and undergrowth while 'leaving the older, thick-barked trees largely unharmed'.

Why not (D): Climbing into the crowns is what the later, large fires did after fuel accumulated; the earlier fires stayed small and on the forest floor.

Common misconception: Confusing the earlier small fires with the later crown fires described in the same paragraph.

Difficulty: Stated in one sentence. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P06-013 · Ecology and conservation · passage Medium · 409 words · 4 questions

The model of the national park that spread across much of the world in the twentieth century rested on a simple picture: nature is most fully itself where people are absent, and the task of conservation is to keep them out. Boundaries were drawn, guards were posted, and in many places the people who had lived within the new boundaries, herding, gathering, farming small plots or hunting, were moved elsewhere. Critics have come to call this approach fortress conservation, and the name captures both its method and its mood.

The picture of untouched nature was often historically false. Many landscapes that visitors admired as wilderness had been shaped for centuries by the people who were then removed: grasslands kept open by grazing and deliberate burning, forests whose composition reflected generations of selective harvesting and planting. Removing those people did not return the land to an original state; it began a new and unplanned experiment, and in some cases the very qualities that had made the place worth protecting began to decline. The costs to the displaced were, of course, more direct: the loss of homes, of access to food and medicine, of graves and sacred places, often with little or no compensation.

It would be too simple, however, to conclude that local residents are always the best stewards and that exclusion is never justified. Communities change, markets reach into forests, and a population that once harvested lightly may, under new pressures, begin to clear or hunt at rates the land cannot sustain. Some species, particularly those that are very sensitive to disturbance, may genuinely need areas where human presence is kept to a minimum. The romantic image of the timeless forest dweller can be as misleading as the image of untouched wilderness that it seeks to replace.

What the critique of fortress conservation establishes, then, is not a rule but a burden of proof. Exclusion imposes certain and heavy costs on identifiable people, while its benefits to wildlife are often assumed rather than shown. Where those who would be displaced have a long record of use compatible with the values being protected, the presumption should lie with their remaining, and with arrangements that give them a real share in managing the land. Where that record is absent, or where a species' needs are demonstrably incompatible with human use, exclusion may be defended; but it must be defended, case by case, rather than taken as the natural form of protection.

  2. (B)Because romantic images of forest dwellers are as misleading as images of wilderness, conservation should rely on neither residents nor parks to protect species.
  3. (C)Because national parks were founded on a false picture of untouched nature, the model should be abandoned and replaced by community management everywhere.
  4. (D)Because local residents shaped many admired landscapes over centuries, they should never be removed from land that is set aside for conservation.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 4 sums up: the critique establishes 'not a rule but a burden of proof'. Exclusion has 'certain and heavy costs' while its benefits 'are often assumed rather than shown', so it 'must be defended, case by case, rather than taken as the natural form of protection'.

Why not (B): Paragraph 3 uses the romantic image only to qualify the critique; the passage keeps both protection and residents' share in management, rejecting neither.

Why not (C): The author concludes with 'not a rule but a burden of proof'; replacing parks with community management everywhere is itself a rule the passage does not propose.

Why not (D): Paragraph 3 rejects the view that 'exclusion is never justified', and paragraph 4 allows exclusion where use is absent or demonstrably incompatible.

Common misconception: Picking the option that matches the critique in paragraph 2 and missing the qualification in paragraph 3.

Difficulty: All options borrow genuine premises from the passage; the correct answer alone keeps the balance of the conclusion. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET
