CAT-VARC-P06-014 · Ecology and conservation · passage Medium · 415 words · 4 questions

The idea of a keystone species borrows its name from architecture. In an arch, the keystone is the wedge at the top that holds the other stones in place; remove it, and the structure falls. Applied to ecosystems, the term picks out species whose effect on their community is far larger than their abundance would suggest, so that their loss sets off changes that ripple through many other species. A predator that keeps a grazer in check, allowing plants to flourish, is the classic case, and the concept has proved enormously useful in persuading the public and governments that the fate of a single species can matter to a whole landscape.

The vividness of the metaphor, however, carries assumptions that ecology does not always support. An arch has one keystone, and its role is fixed by the geometry of the structure. Ecological communities are not like this. The same species may have a dominant influence in one place and a minor one in another, depending on what else lives there, on the climate and on the history of the site. A predator that controls its prey on a rocky shore where that prey has few other enemies may matter much less where the prey is also eaten by several other animals. Keystone status, in other words, is a property of a relationship in a particular setting, not a fixed attribute of a species.

The metaphor also invites a search for the one species that holds everything together, when in many communities influence is spread across several species, each of moderate importance, whose combined effects are what keep the system stable. A conservation strategy built around identifying and protecting keystones may then direct attention to a few dramatic interactions and neglect the diffuse ones, which are harder to demonstrate but not necessarily less important. The absence of a striking keystone in a community does not show that its members are interchangeable.

None of this means the concept should be discarded. Some species really do have outsized and well-documented effects, and naming them helps in arguing for their protection. The danger lies in treating the label as a discovery about a species' nature rather than a finding about a particular system, and in assuming that where no keystone has been identified, no species is indispensable. The architectural image is best used as what it is, a way of making one kind of ecological fact memorable, and not as a model of how communities are generally built.

CAT-VARC-P06-015 · Ecology and conservation · passage Difficult · 581 words · 5 questions

Conservationists have long sorted species into two kinds. Native species belong where they are found and deserve protection; alien species, carried by people beyond their former range, are suspect, and those that spread are labelled invasive and targeted for control. The distinction is so familiar that it is rarely examined, yet it rests on a choice that is not itself ecological. To call a species native is to say that it was present before some date, usually the arrival of a particular wave of human travel or trade, and the date is a convention. Species have always moved, carried by winds, currents and birds or on their own legs, and as the climate warms many are shifting their ranges without any human hand to carry them. A plant that crosses a mountain range by itself is not usually called an invader; one that crosses in a shipment of grain is.

A growing number of ecologists conclude that origin should simply be dropped as a criterion. What matters, they argue, is what a species does. Many introduced species cause no measurable harm, and some are positively useful: they hold soil on eroded slopes, feed pollinators where native flowers have been lost, or provide cover for native birds in landscapes that offer little else. Campaigns to remove such species spend scarce money and can do damage of their own, through herbicides, through disturbance of the ground, or by stripping out shelter on which native animals had come to depend. Judging species by their passports rather than by their effects is, on this view, a kind of ecological nativism, and conservation would do better without it.

The critics are right that origin is not itself a harm, and right that much eradication has been undertaken without asking whether the target was doing any damage. But their alternative assumes that effects can be known at the moment a decision has to be made, and this is often false. Introduced species frequently remain scarce and inconspicuous for decades before something, a change in climate, a new road, the arrival of a suitable pollinator, allows them to spread rapidly. By the time the harm is plain, removal has usually become impossible. Moreover, a newly arrived species has no shared history with the predators, parasites and competitors of its new home, and so its future behaviour there is harder to forecast than that of a species whose relationships have been worked out over a long time. This does not make the newcomer more likely to be harmful; it makes its effects less predictable, which is a different thing.

Seen this way, origin is neither a verdict nor an irrelevance but a piece of evidence, and what it is evidence of is uncertainty. Its weight changes with time. When a species has only just arrived, removal is cheap and its effects are unknown, and a presumption against it is reasonable precisely because the decision cannot wait for the evidence that the critics demand. When a species has been established for a century and its effects can be observed, the presumption has done its work, and the case for removing it should rest on what it is shown to do; the label alien then adds little. Both the defenders of the old distinction and its critics treat the question as one of identity, of what a species is. It is better understood as a question of timing and of knowledge: of what can be known about a species, and when.

CAT-VARC-P06-015-Q1 · Structure / function · MediumMCQ

Why not (A): Speed and distance of spread are never compared; the example sets two plants making the same crossing side by side to show that only the means of travel differs.

Why not (B): Paragraph 1 makes no recommendation about protection; the author's own position, given in paragraph 4, allows a presumption against newly arrived species.

Why not (C): The passage compares no harms in paragraph 1; the example concerns what the plant is called, and paragraph 3 later says even newcomers are not 'more likely to be harmful'.

Common misconception: Reading the example as a claim about harm or speed of spread, which the paragraph does not discuss.

Difficulty: The example's function follows from the preceding sentence about the convention behind native status, but wrong options attach plausible ecological claims to it. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

Why not (C): The author denies this in paragraph 3: the lack of shared history 'does not make the newcomer more likely to be harmful', so it is not what divides the author from the critics.

Why not (D): The author concedes this too, saying the critics are 'right that much eradication has been undertaken without asking whether the target was doing any damage'.

Common misconception: Choosing the option about newcomers being more harmful, which sounds like the traditional view the critics attack but which the author explicitly rejects.

Difficulty: Two wrong options state points the author concedes and one states a view the author rejects; only careful reading of paragraph 3 locates the real disagreement. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P06-016 · Ecology and conservation · passage Difficult · 616 words · 5 questions

Ecological restoration has traditionally worked towards a picture. A degraded marsh or heath is compared with a reference state, usually the condition of the site at some earlier date as reconstructed from records, maps or surviving fragments, and success is measured by how closely the site comes to resemble it. Rewilding, in many of its versions, refuses this. Rather than aiming at a particular assemblage of species, it seeks to restore processes: grazing and browsing by large herbivores, the flooding of rivers that have been confined to channels, predation, the falling and rotting of trees. What the landscape then becomes is left open. Its advocates present this refusal to name an end state not as a gap in the method but as its central virtue.

Their reasons are serious. Any reference state is chosen, not found: a site has passed through many conditions, and to select one of them as the true or original form of the place is to privilege one moment of its history over all the others. Under a changing climate, moreover, the conditions that sustained a historical community may no longer exist, so that holding a landscape to an old picture would require ever more intervention, mowing, planting, culling and weeding on a scale that amounts to gardening, however large the garden. Better, the argument runs, to set processes going and accept whatever community they produce.

The obvious objection is that a project with no target cannot fail. If whatever emerges is by definition what nature has chosen, then no outcome, however disappointing, counts as evidence against the project, and those who fund it, live beside it or simply care about the place are left with no way to hold it to account. The objection becomes sharper when the results are unwelcome: when scrub swallows a flower-rich meadow that people valued, or when a rare species that depended on the old management quietly disappears. To answer that this, too, is nature taking its course is to make the project immune to criticism, and a claim that nothing could count against is not much of a claim.

Advocates have a partial reply. A project can be held to account for its processes even if not for its outcome: one can ask whether the river is in fact allowed to flood, whether herbivore numbers rise and fall with the food available rather than being fixed by managers, whether dead wood is left where it falls. These are checkable commitments, and a project that fails to keep them has failed in a sense that matters. But the reply does not dissolve the difficulty, because the choice of processes is itself a choice about outcomes. Which animals are released, in what numbers, within what fences and over how large an area will go a long way towards deciding whether the land becomes closed woodland, open pasture or something between, and often these consequences can be foreseen. A few cattle and ponies at low density on a large area will produce a different landscape from many of them on a small one, and those who set the density know this.

What rewilding achieves, then, is not the withdrawal of human choice from the landscape but its relocation, from the end of the process to its beginning. This is not an argument against rewilding, which may well be the most sensible response available in a world where no historical picture can be held still. It is an argument against a certain way of describing it. The phrase 'letting nature decide' conceals decisions that people have already made, and the method is more honest, and more open to the scrutiny it needs, when its advocates own those decisions.

CAT-VARC-P06-016-Q1 · Main idea / primary purpose · DifficultMCQ
Which of the following best expresses the main point of the passage?
  1. (A)By declining to name an end state, rewilding does not remove human choice but moves it to the start of the process, which its advocates should admit.
  2. (B)Because a rewilding project without a target can never be shown to have failed, restoration towards a recorded earlier state remains the more accountable method.
  3. (C)Since every reference state is chosen and climate change puts old states out of reach, conservation should restore natural processes and leave the outcome entirely to nature.
  4. (D)Holding rewilding projects to account for the processes they restore, rather than for their outcomes, fully answers the charge that the method cannot fail.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 5 states the conclusion: rewilding achieves 'not the withdrawal of human choice from the landscape but its relocation, from the end of the process to its beginning', and the method is 'more honest' when 'its advocates own those decisions'.

Why not (B): This is the objection of paragraph 3, which the author treats as partly answered; paragraph 5 says the argument 'is not an argument against rewilding' and calls it possibly 'the most sensible response'.

Why not (C): This is the advocates' case from paragraph 2; the author concludes that 'letting nature decide' conceals decisions people have already made, so outcomes are not left entirely to nature.

Why not (D): Paragraph 4 calls this only 'a partial reply' and says it 'does not dissolve the difficulty', because choosing processes is itself a choice about outcomes.

Common misconception: Choosing the advocates' view or the critics' view, each of which the passage presents fairly before qualifying it.

Difficulty: Each wrong option states a position that the passage sets out at length; the correct answer requires following the argument to its last paragraph. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P06-017 · Earth, climate and oceans · passage Easy · 440 words · 4 questions

Few scientific failures are held against a discipline as often as seismology's inability to say when the next large earthquake will strike. The public imagines that a sufficiently clever instrument, placed in the right fault zone, ought eventually to detect a warning sign, much as a barometer falls before a storm. Over the past century researchers have pursued many such signs, from changes in groundwater levels and gas emissions to swarms of small tremors and odd animal behaviour. Each has, at one time or another, appeared to precede a particular earthquake, and each has failed when tested against the many earthquakes that came without it and the many apparent warnings that were followed by nothing.

It is worth separating two activities that ordinary speech runs together. A prediction, in the sense the public wants, names a place, a magnitude and a narrow window of time: a strong earthquake near this town within the next week. A forecast states a probability over a longer period: a given chance of shaking above some level in this region within the next thirty years. The first remains beyond reach, and many seismologists suspect it may stay there, because whether a small rupture grows into a large one seems to depend on conditions deep in the crust that no instrument can survey in detail. The second is routine, and it is far from useless.

Forecasts are what building codes are written from. When engineers decide how strongly a hospital must be braced, or which soils are too loose to build on, they are acting on probabilities of exactly this kind. A city that has absorbed its long-term hazard into the way it builds has, in effect, already received the most valuable warning seismology can give, though nobody experiences it as a warning, since it arrives years before the event and asks for money rather than flight.

The objection that a probability is too vague to act on mistakes the kind of decision at stake. Few people would evacuate a city on the strength of a one-in-twenty chance spread over three decades, and nobody should. But a decision about whether to reinforce a school is not a decision about this week; it is a decision about the lifetime of a building, and over that lifetime a one-in-twenty chance is large. This does not mean that the search for warning signs should be abandoned, since a reliable one would save lives that codes cannot. It means that the discipline should not be judged by its failure at a task it may never accomplish while its success at a quieter task goes unremarked.

CAT-VARC-P06-017-Q1 · Main idea / primary purpose · EasyMCQ
The primary purpose of the passage is to

  3. (C)explain why the conditions deep in the crust that decide the size of an earthquake can never be measured by any instrument.
  4. (D)show that the search for warning signs before earthquakes has been a waste of effort and ought now to be given up.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 1 states the common complaint, paragraph 2 separates prediction from forecasting, paragraph 3 shows that forecasts shape building codes, and paragraph 4 concludes that the discipline 'should not be judged by its failure' at prediction while 'its success at a quieter task goes unremarked'. The purpose is to shift how seismology is judged.

Why not (B): Paragraph 3 says the opposite: 'Forecasts are what building codes are written from', and engineers already act on these probabilities.

Why not (C): Paragraph 2 mentions these conditions only as a likely reason prediction is out of reach, and says seismologists 'suspect' it, not that measurement is impossible; the option is too narrow and too absolute.

Why not (D): Paragraph 4 says 'This does not mean that the search for warning signs should be abandoned', because a reliable sign 'would save lives that codes cannot', so the option reverses the author's position.

Common misconception: Taking the account of failed warning signs in paragraph 1 as the point of the passage, and missing the concession in paragraph 4.

Difficulty: The thesis is stated almost directly in the final sentence; the wrong options are clearly contradicted or too narrow. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P06-018 · Earth, climate and oceans · passage Medium · 425 words · 4 questions

For much of the twentieth century, weather forecasting was imagined as a problem of insufficient detail. If the atmosphere obeyed known physical laws, then better observations and faster calculation should, in principle, allow the future to be computed as far ahead as anyone liked. The recognition that the atmosphere is chaotic, in the technical sense that tiny differences in its starting state grow until they dominate the outcome, seemed at first to end that hope. Beyond a horizon of a week or two, no conceivable improvement in measurement would make a single calculated forecast reliable, because no measurement can be perfect and every imperfection is eventually amplified.

What is striking is how the discipline turned this limit into a method. Instead of running a model once from the best available estimate of the present, forecasting centres now run it dozens of times, each from a starting state nudged slightly within the range of what observations allow. If the runs stay close together, the atmosphere is in a predictable mood and the forecast can be stated with confidence; if they fan out, the spread itself is the information, telling forecasters that several quite different outcomes remain live. Chaos, which looked like a verdict against prediction, became a way of measuring how much prediction a given situation permits.

Much of this gain is lost in the last step, when forecasts reach the public. A phone application that shows a single icon for Thursday, a cloud with three raindrops, has quietly discarded the ensemble and kept only something like its average. Users then judge the forecast by whether it rained, and a forecast that correctly said the outcome was uncertain is remembered as a forecast that was wrong. The habit is understandable, since most people want a decision rather than a distribution, but it trains the public to expect a kind of certainty the science has explicitly given up.

It would be a mistake, however, to treat the ensemble as a perfectly honest witness. Every run in it uses the same model, with the same simplified treatment of clouds, mountains and ocean surfaces, so errors built into the model are shared by all the members and do not show up as spread. An ensemble can therefore agree with itself and still be wrong, and forecasters spend considerable effort checking whether its spread matches the errors actually observed over many past forecasts. The spread is a measure of uncertainty, not a guarantee of it, and its value depends on that continual comparison with what the weather in fact did.

CAT-VARC-P06-018-Q1 · Structure / function · EasyMCQ

  1. (A)show that the models used by phone applications are cruder than the ones that forecasting centres run many times.
  2. (B)show how a single summary symbol throws away the information about uncertainty that running many forecasts provides.
  3. (C)prove that weather forecasts presented to the public turn out to be wrong about rainfall more often than not.
  4. (D)support the view that forecasts ought to be issued to the public only by meteorologists trained in reading ensembles.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 3 opens by saying the gain 'is lost in the last step'. The icon is the illustration: it 'has quietly discarded the ensemble and kept only something like its average', so the spread, which paragraph 2 called 'the information', never reaches users.

Why not (A): The passage says the application 'kept only something like its average', meaning it simplifies the ensemble's output; it says nothing about the application running a different model.

Why not (C): The passage says a correct forecast of uncertainty is 'remembered as' wrong, which concerns how forecasts are judged, not how often they actually fail.

Why not (D): The passage offers no remedy of this kind; paragraph 3 describes a loss and calls the public's habit 'understandable' without recommending who should issue forecasts.

Common misconception: Reading the example as a claim about model quality or accuracy rather than about presentation.

Difficulty: The sentence before the example states the point it illustrates. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P06-018-Q3 · Author's tone / attitude · MediumMCQ
The author's attitude toward ensemble forecasting is best described as
  1. (A)detached: describing how ensembles are run and what they show while declining to judge whether the approach is a good one.
  2. (B)wholly enthusiastic: convinced that running many forecasts has removed the uncertainty that chaos once seemed to impose.
  3. (C)suspicious: inclined to treat the method as an elaborate way of disguising the failure of weather prediction.
  4. (D)appreciative but qualified: impressed that a limit became a method, yet alert to what the method cannot reveal.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 2 praises the conversion of chaos into 'a way of measuring how much prediction a given situation permits', while paragraph 4 warns against treating the ensemble 'as a perfectly honest witness'. Both halves of the correct answer are needed.

Why not (A): Phrases such as 'What is striking' and 'It would be a mistake, however' express clear judgments, so the author is not neutral.

Why not (B): Paragraph 4 says an ensemble 'can therefore agree with itself and still be wrong', and paragraph 2 says the spread measures uncertainty rather than removing it.

Why not (C): The author calls the change 'striking' and says chaos 'became a way of measuring how much prediction a given situation permits', which is praise, not suspicion.

Common misconception: Seizing on either the praise of paragraph 2 or the warning of paragraph 4 alone.

Difficulty: The tone shifts across paragraphs, so the student must weigh praise against qualification. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P06-018-Q4 · Application / analogy · DifficultMCQ
Which of the following situations best illustrates the specific weakness described in paragraph 4?
  1. (A)Every run of an ensemble forecasts a dry week for a valley because the shared model mishandles air rising over nearby hills, and heavy rain falls there.
  2. (B)The runs of an ensemble fan out widely after a few days, and forecasters announce that several quite different outcomes are still possible.
  3. (C)A single forecast run from a slightly inaccurate measurement of today's winds drifts further from reality with each passing day.
  4. (D)A phone application shows a sun for Saturday although about half of the ensemble's runs had forecast showers for that day.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 4 says all runs share the same model, so 'errors built into the model are shared by all the members and do not show up as spread'; an ensemble 'can therefore agree with itself and still be wrong'. In the correct answer, the runs agree because of a shared mistake about hills, one of the features the paragraph names, and are wrong together.

Why not (B): This is the method working as paragraph 2 describes: the spread 'is the information'. No hidden error is involved.

Why not (C): This is the chaotic amplification of starting errors in paragraph 1, which ensembles are designed to handle; it is not an error shared by every member.

Why not (D): This is the presentation problem of paragraph 3, where the ensemble is reduced to 'something like its average', not a weakness of the ensemble itself.

Common misconception: Choosing the option that matches another paragraph's problem (chaos, presentation, wide spread), since each of those options is a faithful picture of something the passage discusses.

Difficulty: Each wrong option accurately illustrates a different paragraph; only the correct answer shows agreement produced by a shared model error. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P06-019 · Earth, climate and oceans · passage Medium · 431 words · 4 questions

Among the records of past climate, ice cores enjoy a special prestige. Drilled from the great ice sheets of Greenland and Antarctica, they contain small bubbles of air sealed off as snow was buried and compressed, so that a scientist can, in a sense, hold in a laboratory a sample of the atmosphere as it was long before any human being farmed. Tree rings, lake sediments and the shells of tiny marine organisms all have to be translated into climate through some chain of reasoning. The bubbles in ice seem to need no translation at all: the gas is simply there, waiting to be measured.

The appearance of directness is partly an illusion, and an instructive one. Snow does not seal itself the moment it falls. For decades, centuries or, at the driest sites, far longer, depending on how quickly snow accumulates, the upper layer remains porous, and air from the surface continues to mix downward through it. By the time the bubbles finally close, the gas they hold is considerably younger than the ice that surrounds it. To date the air, then, researchers must model how compaction proceeds at a given site, which in turn depends on temperature and snowfall, the very quantities the core is being used to reconstruct. Temperature itself is not read off the ice but inferred from the proportions of heavier and lighter forms of water, a relationship calibrated against modern observations and assumed to have held in the past.

None of this makes the records untrustworthy. It does, however, change where their authority comes from. A core is persuasive not because it bypasses interpretation but because its interpretations can be checked against one another and against independent archives. Traces of very large volcanic eruptions, which can appear in cores drilled at opposite ends of the planet, allow records to be aligned; the timing of changes in ice can be compared with changes recorded in ocean sediments, whose dating rests on quite different assumptions. When such independent lines agree, the agreement is far harder to explain away than any single measurement, however direct it seems.

The point matters beyond the laboratory. Public arguments about climate often treat evidence as either raw and therefore reliable or interpreted and therefore suspect. Ice cores show why this division is unhelpful. Almost all scientific evidence is interpreted; what distinguishes strong evidence from weak is whether its interpretations are exposed to independent tests that could have failed. A measurement trusted because it seemed to speak for itself would deserve less confidence than one whose inferences had been stated openly and checked.

CAT-VARC-P06-019-Q1 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ
According to the passage, the air in an ice core's bubbles is younger than the surrounding ice because

  2. (B)fresh air seeps into the ice through cracks that open when a core is drilled and lifted to the surface.
  3. (C)the top layer of snow stays porous for a long time, letting surface air mix downward until the bubbles seal.
  4. (D)summer melting each year reworks the ice and traps air from later seasons in layers of older snow.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 2: 'the upper layer remains porous, and air from the surface continues to mix downward through it. By the time the bubbles finally close, the gas they hold is considerably younger than the ice'.

Why not (A): Paragraph 2 says air mixes in before 'the bubbles finally close'; nothing says air is forced into ice that had already sealed.

Why not (B): The passage never mentions contamination during drilling; it explains the age difference by the porous upper layer that stays open, as paragraph 2 puts it, for 'decades, centuries or, at the driest sites, far longer'.

Why not (D): Melting is not mentioned anywhere; paragraph 2 attributes the difference to air mixing 'downward' through porous snow.

Common misconception: Supplying a plausible physical mechanism the passage does not give.

Difficulty: The mechanism is stated in two consecutive sentences of paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P06-020 · Earth, climate and oceans · passage Easy · 432 words · 4 questions

When the sea threatens a coastal town, the first response is almost always to defend it. Walls are raised, beaches are replenished with sand pumped from offshore, and the line between land and water is held where it has always been. To propose instead that some places should be gradually abandoned sounds like defeatism, a confession that the town has been given up for lost. Yet the choice is rarely between holding the line and surrendering it. More often it is between retreat that is planned and retreat that is forced.

Defences are not permanent solutions but commitments that grow. A wall built to withstand the storms of today must be raised as the sea rises, and every raising protects more property that was built because the previous wall made it seem safe. Each round of investment makes the next one harder to refuse, since abandoning the wall would now mean abandoning more. Engineers can usually design a higher barrier; the deeper difficulty is that the community sheltering behind it has, by then, organised its entire economic life around the expectation that the barrier will hold. In low-lying places the expense eventually outruns what the community can pay, and the decision to leave is then made not by a council in calm weather but by the storm that finally breaks through.

Retreat imposed in that way is the worst version of a policy that might have been tolerable. Households leave in a rush, with property that has lost its value overnight, and they scatter wherever they can find housing, so that the neighbourhood that made the place worth defending dissolves. Planned retreat, by contrast, can be slow. A town can stop permitting new building in the most exposed areas, offer to buy homes when owners choose to sell, and move a school or a clinic inland when it would have needed rebuilding anyway. Spread over decades, such steps let a community move as a community.

None of this means that every shore should be abandoned. Some coasts carry so much population and infrastructure that defending them is plainly worth the cost, and in poor places the money for orderly relocation is often lacking. Nor can retreat simply be ordered from above; people who have lived beside the water for generations have reasons for staying that no compensation formula captures, and a plan that ignores them will fail. The argument is narrower: retreat belongs among the options from the start, weighed openly alongside walls, rather than held back as an admission of failure until it can no longer be chosen at all.

Why not (A): Residents' attachment in paragraph 4 is to living 'beside the water', not to the wall; paragraph 2 explains the difficulty by the property the wall protects.

Why not (C): Paragraph 2 says nothing about growing engineering confidence; the reason given is that 'abandoning the wall would now mean abandoning more'.

Common misconception: Supplying a plausible but unstated reason, or misplacing the residents' attachment described in paragraph 4.

Difficulty: The reason is spelt out in two adjacent sentences. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P06-021 · Earth, climate and oceans · passage Medium · 442 words · 4 questions

Each summer, the arrival of the monsoon over the southern tip of the Indian peninsula is announced on a particular day, and the date is reported as if something had been observed to happen: the rains have come. Newspapers compare it with the long-term average, and a delay of a week becomes a story about anxious farmers and rising food prices. Behind the announcement, however, lies not an event but a decision. The monsoon has no sharp leading edge. Showers build over days or weeks, winds shift gradually, and early bursts of rain may be followed by long dry spells. To fix a single day, forecasters must apply criteria, thresholds of rainfall across a set of stations and of wind and cloud, chosen so that the date can be determined consistently from one year to the next.

Calling the onset a convention is not to call it arbitrary. Good criteria pick out a real change in the behaviour of the atmosphere, and fixing them in advance prevents the date from being adjusted to suit hopes or headlines. A consistent definition also makes years comparable, so that researchers can ask whether onsets have tended to come earlier or later over many decades. Conventions of this kind are the ordinary machinery of science; the point at which a heavy shower counts as an extreme rainfall event, or a tropical storm as a cyclone, is fixed in the same way.

The trouble begins when the convention is mistaken for the thing it marks. A declared onset tells a farmer that a threshold was crossed somewhere along the coast; it does not tell her whether the soil in her own district holds enough moisture for seed to survive the next fortnight. In some years the rains, having satisfied the criteria, stall for weeks, and farmers who sowed on the strength of the announcement watch their seedlings wither. The date did nothing wrong; it was simply asked to answer a question it was never designed to answer.

The lesson is not that the onset date should be abolished, since it serves research and public communication reasonably well, but that a single number cannot serve every purpose at once. A measure built for comparing years across a century need not be the measure that should guide a decision about sowing next week. Agricultural advice would be better anchored in local indicators, such as accumulated rainfall and soil moisture in the district concerned, with the national date treated as background rather than as a signal to act. Definitions in climate science, like definitions elsewhere, should be judged by whether they fit the decision they are used to inform.

CAT-VARC-P06-022 · Earth, climate and oceans · passage Medium · 401 words · 4 questions

Among researchers who study catastrophes, a slogan has become something close to a creed: there is no such thing as a natural disaster. The claim is not that earthquakes, floods and cyclones are human inventions. It is that a hazard, a physical event of a certain size, becomes a disaster only when it meets people and structures unable to withstand it. A cyclone that crosses an empty coastline is a fact about the atmosphere; the same cyclone striking a crowded delta of unreinforced houses with no warning system is a social event, and the scale of its damage is decided as much by the delta's history as by the storm.

The slogan earns its popularity because it redirects attention to what can be changed. Two cities shaken by earthquakes of similar strength may suffer wholly different losses, one counting broken windows and the other thousands of dead, and the difference lies in building standards, in whether they were enforced, in where the poor were permitted or forced to live. Calling the outcome natural lets those decisions disappear from view. It encourages the belief that loss is fate, and fate calls for sympathy after the event rather than investment before it.

Taken literally, however, the slogan claims too much. Some events are large enough that no plausible arrangement of society would have prevented great loss: a sufficiently large eruption, or a wave of extraordinary height arriving within minutes, can overwhelm even well-prepared places. To insist that every such loss was socially produced is to imply that someone could have prevented it, which may be false, and in its cruder versions comes close to suggesting that the people harmed were responsible for their own exposure. The slogan can also tempt its users to neglect the physical science of hazards, as though knowing how often floods of a given size recur mattered less than studying the societies they strike, when the two kinds of knowledge are needed together.

The insight is worth keeping and the absolutism worth dropping. A more defensible claim is that the size of a disaster is never simply natural: the hazard sets the challenge, but how much of the challenge becomes catastrophe depends on choices made long before. This version keeps the slogan's critical edge, its refusal to let preventable deaths be filed under bad luck, while admitting that nature sometimes sets challenges that no society could fully meet.

CAT-VARC-P06-022-Q1 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ

  2. (B)is an event that scientists can forecast in advance, whereas a disaster is one that arrives without any warning at all.
  3. (C)is caused by human activity such as building on deltas, whereas a disaster has wholly natural causes such as storms.
  4. (D)affects only empty coastlines and open country, whereas a disaster is confined to cities with many unreinforced houses.

Why not (B): Forecastability is not the distinction drawn; paragraph 1 mentions a missing 'warning system' only as one feature of a vulnerable delta.

Why not (C): Paragraph 1 says the opposite: the hazard is 'a physical event', and the disaster depends on the society it strikes.

Common misconception: Turning the illustration of the empty coastline into a definition.

Difficulty: The definition is stated in one sentence. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P06-022-Q2 · Inference · MediumMCQ
The author suggests that describing a disaster's outcome as natural tends to
  1. (A)lead the people harmed to blame themselves for having chosen to live in places exposed to known hazards.
  2. (B)encourage scientists to study how often hazards of a given size recur instead of examining the societies they strike.
  3. (C)push governments to enforce building standards more strictly in cities that have already suffered heavy losses.
  4. (D)move public effort away from prevention beforehand and toward sympathy once the damage is done.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 2: calling the outcome natural 'encourages the belief that loss is fate, and fate calls for sympathy after the event rather than investment before it'.

Why not (A): Paragraph 3 attributes something like this to 'cruder versions' of the slogan, the opposite framing, and speaks of suggestion, not of victims' own beliefs.

Why not (B): Paragraph 3 says the slogan, not the natural framing, might lead people to neglect the physical science; the option reverses the source of the bias.

Why not (C): Paragraph 2 says calling the outcome natural 'lets those decisions disappear from view', which would weaken pressure for enforcement, not increase it.

Common misconception: Attaching the slogan's risks from paragraph 3 to the opposite framing discussed in paragraph 2.

Difficulty: Two wrong options are drawn from paragraph 3 and must be traced to the right framing. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P06-023 · Earth, climate and oceans · passage Difficult · 587 words · 5 questions

Geologists divide the history of the Earth into named intervals, and they are unusually strict about where one interval ends and the next begins. A boundary is not fixed by a date written in a textbook but by a physical reference point: a particular layer in a particular sequence of rock or sediment, chosen because it records a change that can be recognised in deposits across the globe, and marked, in the profession's half-joking phrase, by a golden spike. The rigour has a purpose. The time scale exists so that a geologist examining strata on one continent can say with confidence that they formed at the same time as strata on another, and a boundary that cannot be traced from place to place is of little use for that task. Precision about where a boundary lies is, in this sense, the price paid for precision about when.

The proposal that we now live in a new interval, the Anthropocene, defined by the imprint of human activity on the planet, has therefore been argued in unusual terms. Much of the debate has concerned not whether humans have transformed the Earth, a point few specialists now dispute, but where the spike should go. Candidates have ranged from the spread of farming thousands of years ago, through the industrial burning of coal, to the middle of the twentieth century, when fallout from nuclear weapons tests left a thin but nearly worldwide trace in sediments and ice. The later dates are favoured partly because they are sharp. A signal laid down within a few years everywhere is exactly what a stratigrapher wants; the slow spread of farming, which reached different regions in different millennia, is not.

Here the demands of the time scale begin to distort the story they are meant to record. Human influence on the Earth did not arrive at one moment. Forests were cleared, soils eroded and large animals hunted to extinction over thousands of years, at different times on different continents, and the acceleration of the last century was the culmination of that history rather than its beginning. Choosing the mid-twentieth century because its markers are crisp risks implying that the transformation began then, and that everything before it belongs to an untouched natural world. A boundary drawn there would be accurate about the rocks and misleading about the long human history that produced them. The criterion that makes a boundary useful for correlating rocks is not the criterion that makes it faithful to a process that was, by its nature, gradual and uneven.

None of this shows that formal definition is pointless. A ratified boundary would give researchers in many fields a common reference, and it would carry an authority that informal usage lacks. Some defenders of formalisation fear that without it the idea will remain a slogan, borrowed by commentators and never disciplined by evidence. But the concept does not need a spike to do its intellectual work. The claim that human activity has become a force comparable to the processes that build mountains and shift climates rests on measurements of carbon, sediment, species and nitrogen, and those measurements remain what they are whatever any committee decides. A concept can be rigorous without being a unit of the time scale; geologists already speak, informally, of great events that unfolded over long and varying spans. The Anthropocene may be better understood in that way, as a transformation with a long and diachronous history, rather than as an epoch that began in a particular year.

  2. (B)geologists insist on physical reference points for boundaries because the time scale must let strata on different continents be matched.
  3. (C)the idea of the Anthropocene is a slogan used by commentators, and it will gain scientific standing only once a boundary is formally ratified.
  4. (D)the criteria for a formal geological boundary sit poorly with the history of human influence, which does not need such a boundary to be taken seriously.

Paragraph 3 says the demands of the time scale 'distort the story they are meant to record', and paragraph 4 concludes that 'the concept does not need a spike to do its intellectual work'. The correct answer combines both claims.

Why not (A): Paragraph 2 reports that later dates are favoured for their sharpness, but paragraph 3 says choosing them 'risks implying that the transformation began then'; the author does not endorse this placement.

Why not (B): This is true of paragraph 1 but too narrow: it describes the background against which the argument of paragraphs 3 and 4 is made, not the argument itself.

Why not (C): This is the fear of 'Some defenders of formalisation' in paragraph 4, which the author answers with 'But the concept does not need a spike to do its intellectual work'.

CAT-VARC-P06-024 · Earth, climate and oceans · passage Difficult · 601 words · 5 questions

The founders of modern geology won their central argument so completely that its terms are now remembered mainly as a caricature. Against older accounts that explained mountains, valleys and buried shells by a few vast upheavals, they proposed that the Earth's features had been shaped by the same slow processes that can be watched today: rivers cutting their beds, waves wearing down cliffs, sediment settling grain by grain on the floors of lakes and seas. Given enough time, such ordinary causes could account for extraordinary results, and the immense age of the Earth that this view required became one of its most consequential implications. The doctrine came to be called uniformitarianism, and its rival, the appeal to sudden and violent change, catastrophism. In the familiar telling, patient observation defeated speculation.

The telling is unfair to both sides. Uniformitarianism bundled together several claims of very different standing. One was that the laws of nature have not changed, so that water flowed and rock fractured in the past as it does now. Another was methodological: that a geologist should first try to explain the record by causes known to operate, and invoke unobserved ones only when those fail. A third was a claim about the Earth itself, that change had generally proceeded at gentle and roughly steady rates. The first two are close to the preconditions of any historical science. The third is an empirical hypothesis, and it could have turned out false.

In part, it did. Through much of the twentieth century, geologists who proposed sudden events of enormous scale met resistance out of proportion to their evidence. A landscape of deep channels and giant ripples in the north-western United States was long attributed to ordinary erosion before it was accepted as the work of floods released when ice dams failed; the suggestion that the mass extinction at the end of the age of dinosaurs followed the impact of a large asteroid met at first with widespread doubt, some of it dismissive. In both cases the objection was less that the evidence was weak than that the explanation sounded like the discredited catastrophism of the past. A rule of method, prefer known causes, had hardened into a belief about what the Earth was permitted to have done.

It would be wrong, however, to conclude that the old suspicion of catastrophes was mere prejudice. Many early catastrophist explanations could not be tested: they posited events of unspecified cause that left no trace except the very features they were invoked to explain. Against such accounts, a demand for known causes was a reasonable discipline, and it is part of why the later catastrophes were eventually accepted. The flood hypothesis prevailed when the lake held back by the ice and the shorelines it left were identified; the impact hypothesis prevailed when a thin layer rich in a rare metal was traced around the world and a buried crater of the right age was found. What persuaded geologists was not a relaxation of the old standard but evidence that met it.

The view that now prevails keeps the uniformity of laws and the preference for testable causes while abandoning the assumption of uniform rates. The Earth's history includes long stretches of slow change punctuated by rare events of great violence, and both are explained by the same physics; neither the slow nor the violent is an exception that must apologise for itself. The lesson reaches beyond geology. A principle adopted to keep inquiry honest can, if it is mistaken for a description of the world, end by closing questions it was meant to keep open.

  4. (D)the proposals resembled a style of explanation that geologists had long since learned to distrust.

Paragraph 3: 'the objection was less that the evidence was weak than that the explanation sounded like the discredited catastrophism of the past'.

Why not (A): Paragraph 3 says 'the objection was less that the evidence was weak than that the explanation sounded like the discredited catastrophism of the past'.

Why not (B): Paragraph 2 states the rule as invoking unobserved causes 'only when those fail', which permits them; the error was the rule hardening into a belief, not the rule itself.

Why not (C): Nothing suggests either hypothesis broke the uniformity of laws; paragraph 5 says slow change and violent events 'are explained by the same physics', and paragraph 3 locates the resistance in resemblance to old catastrophism.

Common misconception: Assuming that scientists resist ideas chiefly for lack of evidence, the very reason the paragraph sets aside.

Difficulty: The reason is given in one sentence, but two wrong options offer reasonable-sounding alternatives drawn from paragraphs 2 and 4. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P06-025 · Physics, cosmos and measurement · passage Easy · 484 words · 4 questions

Few observations seem less in need of explanation than the darkness of the night sky. The sun sets and the heavens go dark; what else would they do? Yet for several centuries astronomers who took seriously the idea of an infinite, unchanging cosmos found that darkness genuinely puzzling, and their reasoning deserves to be reconstructed, because its premises once seemed almost beyond question. Suppose that stars are scattered more or less uniformly through boundless space. Then any direction in which an observer looks, pursued far enough, must sooner or later meet the bright disc of a star. Remote stars certainly look dimmer than nearby ones, but the number of stars at each successive distance grows in step with the dimming, so that every shell of space contributes as much light as the one inside it. On these assumptions the heavens should glow in all directions with the brilliance of the solar surface.

The first replies tried to rescue the infinite universe by blocking the light. Perhaps clouds of dust between the stars absorbed the glow of the most distant ones. The suggestion sounds reasonable until one follows it through, for dust that absorbs light for long enough is warmed by it, and eventually it glows as brightly as the light it swallows. Over an unlimited span of time, absorption delays the problem; it does not remove it. Other proposals arranged the stars in clusters within clusters, so that lines of sight could slip between them, but these seemed contrived, a geometry invented to answer a single awkward question.

The resolution now accepted is both simpler and stranger. The universe we can observe has a finite age, and light, however swift, needs time to cross space. Most directions therefore never terminate on a star, because the glow of sufficiently remote stars is still in transit, and to look far enough out is to look back to an epoch before any stars had formed. The expansion of space contributes a second effect, stretching the light of faraway sources to longer and feebler wavelengths. Neither point needs to be accepted merely because it dissolves the paradox; each is supported by independent lines of evidence gathered long after the puzzle was first posed.

It would be an exaggeration to say that the darkness of the night proves that the universe had a beginning. Taken alone, it rules out only one combination of assumptions, namely that space is infinite, evenly stocked with stars and unchanging forever, but that is no small thing. A fact available to anyone who steps outside after sunset turns out to be incompatible with what was, for a long time, the most natural picture of the cosmos. The lesson is less about stars than about observation, for the most ordinary features of experience are not always the least informative; sometimes they seem ordinary only because we have stopped asking why they are as they are.

CAT-VARC-P06-025-Q1 · Main idea / primary purpose · MediumMCQ
The passage is primarily concerned with
  1. (A)arguing that ordinary observations are in general more informative about the cosmos than the results of specialised instruments.
  2. (B)showing how an everyday fact, the darkness of the night sky, counts decisively against one long-favoured picture of the universe.
  3. (C)tracing how astronomers used clouds of interstellar dust to defend the idea of an infinite universe against an awkward observation.
  4. (D)proving that the universe had a beginning by showing that every rival explanation of the dark night sky has failed under scrutiny.

Paragraph 1 sets out why an infinite, evenly stocked, unchanging universe predicts a bright sky; paragraph 2 rejects attempts to block the light; paragraph 3 gives the accepted resolution; paragraph 4 concludes that the dark sky 'rules out only one combination of assumptions' but that this 'is no small thing'. The passage is about what this everyday fact tells us.

Why not (C): The dust reply occupies only part of paragraph 2 and is quickly rejected; the option takes one rejected response for the purpose of the whole passage.

Why not (D): Paragraph 4 says outright that it 'would be an exaggeration to say that the darkness of the night proves that the universe had a beginning'; the option claims what the author disowns.

Common misconception: Overstating the conclusion into a proof of a beginning, or mistaking the dust reply for the main subject.

Difficulty: One wrong option turns the hedged conclusion into a proof, another overgeneralises the closing lesson; both use the passage's own words. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P06-025-Q2 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ
According to the passage, interstellar dust cannot resolve the puzzle of the dark sky because
  1. (A)the dust between the stars is spread far too thinly to absorb any significant share of the light of distant stars.
  2. (B)clouds of dust would hide nearby stars as well as distant ones, making the sky far darker than it is actually observed to be.
  3. (C)dust absorbs only some wavelengths of starlight, so that the remaining wavelengths still reach the earth unhindered.
  4. (D)over an unlimited time the dust would be heated by the light it absorbs until it shone as brightly itself.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Why not (C): Nothing is said about dust absorbing selected wavelengths; the only wavelength point, in paragraph 3, concerns the expansion of space.

CAT-VARC-P06-026 · Physics, cosmos and measurement · passage Easy · 413 words · 4 questions

The usual story of standard time is a story of order arriving to rescue confusion. Before the railways, we are told, every town kept its own time, set by the sun at its highest point, so that a clock in one town might differ by several minutes from a clock in another only a short distance to the west. Travellers were bewildered, timetables were impossible, and the adoption of uniform zones was simply common sense finally asserting itself. The story is not false, but it misdescribes what was replaced.

Local solar time was not a muddle for the people who used it. It was tied to something everyone could see: the sun reached its highest point at noon, and noon meant the same thing in the market, the place of worship and the field. For a community whose dealings were mostly with itself, there was little need to coordinate with anyone else, and a time that matched the daylight was the most sensible time to keep. The differences between towns became a problem only when goods and people began to move between them quickly enough for a few minutes to matter.

That is what the railways did. A train leaving one town and arriving at another an hour later could not be scheduled sensibly if each station kept its own noon, and on single tracks a mismatch of a few minutes could put two trains on the same stretch of line. Railway companies therefore began keeping a single time along their routes, and governments eventually followed them, dividing territories into broad bands within which every clock agreed. The change was not everywhere welcomed, and some towns resented being told that noon now arrived when a distant company declared that it had. Within each band, clock noon and solar noon could now differ noticeably, and in places at the edges of a zone they still do.

Nothing here suggests that standard time was a mistake. Coordinated schedules saved lives on the railways and made later systems, from telegraph networks to air traffic, workable. The point is that standardisation was not the discovery of a correct time hidden beneath local error. It was a choice to make clocks answer to a network rather than to the sky, made because the network had become more important to daily life than the position of the sun. To call the older arrangement confusion is to judge it by the needs of a world that had not yet arrived.

  4. (D)governments began to require one uniform time for keeping official records and collecting taxes.

Why not (B): Seasonal variation of noon is never mentioned; paragraph 2 ties the problem to faster movement between towns.

Why not (D): Records and taxes are not discussed, and paragraph 3 says governments 'followed' the railways rather than starting the change.

Common misconception: Reaching for an outside reason, or reversing the order in which railways and governments acted.

Difficulty: The answer is a close paraphrase of one sentence. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P06-027 · Physics, cosmos and measurement · passage Medium · 416 words · 4 questions

For more than a century, the kilogram was a thing. Every measurement of mass in the world, from a pharmacist's dose to a shipping manifest, was ultimately traceable to a single cylinder of platinum and iridium kept under glass near Paris, against which official copies were periodically compared. When the definition was changed in 2019, so that the kilogram is now fixed by assigning an exact value to a fundamental constant of nature, the change was widely reported as the end of arbitrariness: the unit no longer depended on a lump of metal that someone had happened to cast.

That description gets the gain slightly wrong. The new definition is no less conventional in one obvious respect: the value assigned to the constant was chosen precisely so that the new kilogram would match the old one as closely as instruments could tell. Nobody wanted bags of flour to change weight overnight. Had the cylinder been cast a little heavier, the constant would have been given a different number. What the redefinition removed was not human choice but dependence on a particular object, and with it a peculiar kind of blindness.

The blindness worked like this. When the official copies were compared with the cylinder over the decades, they were found to have drifted apart from it by small but measurable amounts. Either the copies had gained mass, or the cylinder had lost it, or both; but because the cylinder was the kilogram by definition, there was no way, even in principle, to say that it had changed. A standard that defines itself cannot be found wanting. Whatever happened to it, it remained, officially, exactly one kilogram, and the masses of everything else silently moved with it. The world's weights were, in effect, being checked against a ruler that nothing could ever check.

A unit tied to a constant does not have this problem. Any sufficiently equipped laboratory can realise the kilogram from the definition, without travelling to a vault, and if two realisations disagree, the disagreement is a fact about the apparatus rather than about the unit. It must be admitted that the apparatus in question is elaborate, and only a handful of institutions can build it; in practice, most of the world still calibrates against physical masses, now traced to these laboratories rather than to Paris. Dependence has not disappeared. But it has become dependence on a procedure that can be checked and repeated, rather than on an object whose changes no one could ever detect.

  3. (C)so that the new kilogram would agree with the old one as closely as instruments could tell.
  4. (D)to match the average mass of the official copies, which were thought more reliable than the cylinder.
Answer and explanation

Why not (A): Paragraph 2 says the value was fixed precisely to 'match the old one', so earlier measurements were the reference.

Why not (B): Paragraph 3 says there was 'no way, even in principle, to say that it had changed', so no known loss could be corrected for.

Why not (D): The passage never ranks the copies above the cylinder; paragraph 2 speaks of matching 'the old one', the cylinder's kilogram.

Common misconception: Assuming the redefinition was meant to fix a known drift.

Difficulty: Stated directly in one sentence of paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P06-028 · Physics, cosmos and measurement · passage Medium · 447 words · 4 questions

It is tempting to treat discarded scientific theories as simple errors, interesting only as warnings. The caloric theory, which held that heat is a subtle, weightless fluid flowing from hotter bodies into colder ones, is a favourite example. We now understand heat in terms of the disordered motion of the particles that make up matter, and nothing like a heat fluid exists. Yet to file caloric under mistakes is to miss what made it, for several decades, the most useful account of heat available, and to misunderstand how the theory that replaced it came to be built.

The fluid picture explained a great deal, and explained it naturally. It said why heat moves from hot to cold, as water runs downhill, and why a hot body and a cold one placed together settle at a common temperature. It made heat something that could be counted, conserved and tracked through a process, which encouraged careful measurements of how much heat different substances absorbed as they warmed or melted. Some of the most enduring results of early thermal science were first obtained by investigators who believed in caloric, and an influential analysis of steam engines, which showed that the work an engine can extract depends on the temperatures between which it operates, was framed entirely in its terms. That result survived the theory's collapse.

The difficulties were real, and they were noticed early. Observations of cannon being bored suggested that friction could produce heat, apparently without limit, for as long as the boring continued. If heat were a conserved fluid squeezed out of the metal, the supply should eventually have run out. Defenders of caloric had replies, some ingenious and some strained, and the theory survived the objection for decades. It is easy to call this stubbornness. It is more accurate to say that a theory which organises a large body of successful work is not abandoned because of one anomaly, particularly when no rival yet explains everything the old theory explained.

None of this is to say that false theories are as good as true ones, or that caloric deserved to last forever. It did, in the end, mislead: it could make no sense of heat turning into work and work into heat, which became central once engines were studied closely. The claim is narrower. A theory can be wrong about what something is while being right about many of the ways it behaves, and those behavioural truths can pass, nearly intact, to its successor. Judging past science only by whether its picture of reality was correct makes its history look like a series of failures, when much of it was a series of partial successes handed on.

Common misconception: Merging the engine example of paragraph 2 with the engine-related failure of paragraph 4.

CAT-VARC-P06-029 · Physics, cosmos and measurement · passage Medium · 504 words · 4 questions

Astronomers cannot lay a measuring tape across the sky, and almost every distance beyond the nearest stars is therefore inferred rather than measured. The method is usually described as a ladder. At the bottom are stars close enough for their apparent shift against the background, as the earth travels around the sun, to be observed and converted into a distance by simple geometry. Among those stars are certain pulsating ones whose rhythm of brightening and fading is tied to their true brightness, so that once the rhythm has been timed, a comparison of true with apparent brightness yields a distance. Such stars can be picked out in nearby galaxies, where they in turn calibrate brighter markers still, among them a class of exploding stars that can be seen across a large part of the observable universe.

Critics of the ladder, and many popular accounts of it, dwell on its fragility. Every rung rests on the one beneath, so an error at the bottom is carried, and perhaps magnified, all the way to the top. A small mistake in the geometry of nearby stars would misplace the pulsating stars, which would misplace the exploding ones, which would in turn misplace the remote galaxies whose distances tell us how fast the universe is expanding. On this view the ladder is really a chain, and a chain is only as strong as its weakest link.

The image is apt, but only half of it. A chain fails at its weakest link because each link is tested by nothing except the load it bears. The rungs of the distance ladder are tested by one another. Wherever two methods can both be applied to the same objects, their answers ought to agree, and when they do not, the disagreement points to a fault in one of them. Several of the largest revisions of cosmic distances in the past century came about in just this way, when a comparison between overlapping rungs exposed a hidden assumption, such as the neglect of dust that dims starlight or the confusion of two kinds of pulsating star with one. Interdependence, in other words, is not only a route by which errors travel upward; it is also the means by which they are caught.

This is no reason for complacency. Cross-checks catch only the errors that differ between methods; a mistake shared by every rung, or built into a calibration on which all of them rely, would pass each comparison untouched. That is why the present disagreement between the expansion rate measured with the ladder and the rate inferred from observations of the early universe is taken so seriously. It may expose a flaw common to the ladder's rungs, or it may reveal something new about the cosmos itself. Either outcome would be informative, and the fact that the question can be posed so sharply is itself a tribute to how far the ladder has been tightened. A structure that could not be checked could never have produced a disagreement worth arguing about.

CAT-VARC-P06-029-Q1 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ
According to the passage, the pulsating stars can serve as measures of distance because
  1. (A)their shift against the background as the earth travels around the sun can be converted into a distance.
  2. (B)they shine so brilliantly when they explode that they can be seen across much of the universe.
  3. (C)their light, unlike that of ordinary stars, is not dimmed by the dust lying between the stars.
  4. (D)the timing of their cycle of brightening and fading reveals how bright they truly are.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 1: their 'rhythm of brightening and fading is tied to their true brightness, so that once the rhythm has been timed, a comparison of true with apparent brightness yields a distance'.

Why not (A): Paragraph 1 gives that geometric method for the nearest stars, the bottom rung; the pulsating stars are useful because their rhythm 'is tied to their true brightness'.

Why not (B): That description belongs to the 'class of exploding stars' on the next rung, which the pulsating stars calibrate, not to the pulsating stars themselves.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 mentions dust only as an assumption once neglected; nothing says pulsating stars are exempt from its dimming.

Common misconception: Attaching to the pulsating stars a property the passage gives to a different rung of the ladder.

Difficulty: The answer is stated in one sentence of paragraph 1; two wrong options borrow details from neighbouring rungs. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P06-029-Q2 · Meaning in context · MediumMCQ
By saying that the chain image is apt 'but only half of it' (paragraph 3), the author means that
  1. (A)the image fits the lower rungs of the ladder well but fails for the upper rungs that rely on exploding stars.
  2. (B)only about half of the rungs rest on the rungs beneath them, while the others are measured independently.
  3. (C)the rungs do depend on one another, but that dependence also lets them test one another, as links cannot.
  4. (D)the ladder is far stronger than its weakest rung, because errors made at the bottom are never carried upward.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 3 accepts the part of the image about dependence and rejects the rest: a chain's links are 'tested by nothing except the load', whereas 'The rungs of the distance ladder are tested by one another'. Interdependence carries errors upward but also catches them.

Why not (A): The passage draws no line between lower and upper rungs; its objection is that the rungs check one another, which a chain's links do not.

Why not (B): This reads 'half' as a count of rungs; the passage says every rung 'rests on the one beneath' and uses 'half' of the image, not of the ladder.

Why not (D): The author grants that interdependence is 'a route by which errors travel upward'; denying that errors are carried upward contradicts paragraph 3.

CAT-VARC-P06-029-Q3 · Inference · MediumMCQ
The passage suggests that the discovery that two kinds of pulsating star had been taken for one
  1. (A)showed that the ladder as a whole is too unreliable to be used in measuring how fast the universe expands.
  2. (B)was made by geometry alone, without any appeal to the other methods by which astronomers measure distance.
  3. (C)is an instance of an error common to every rung, of the kind that comparisons between methods could not catch.

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 3 says several large revisions came about 'when a comparison between overlapping rungs exposed a hidden assumption', naming 'the confusion of two kinds of pulsating star with one' as an example. It was caught because methods applied to the same objects disagreed.

Why not (A): The author cites such revisions as evidence that the ladder corrects itself, and paragraph 4 treats its expansion rate as worth taking 'so seriously'.

Why not (B): The passage presents it as the product of comparing methods, not of a single method working on its own.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 offers it as an error exposed by 'a comparison between overlapping rungs'; paragraph 4 says shared errors 'pass each comparison untouched', so this reverses its role.

CAT-VARC-P06-029-Q4 · Strengthen / weaken / assumption · DifficultMCQ
The claim in paragraph 3 that interdependence is 'the means by which they are caught' depends on which of the following assumptions?
  1. (A)Methods that can be applied to the same objects nearly always give answers that agree within their stated uncertainties.
  2. (B)At least some of the errors that affect one method of finding distances do not also affect another method used on the same objects.
  3. (C)The geometric measurements of the nearest stars are free from any error that could be carried up the ladder.
  4. (D)The expansion rate inferred from the early universe is more trustworthy than the rate found with the ladder.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

A comparison can expose a fault only if the fault does not afflict both methods alike. Paragraph 4 makes the point explicit: 'Cross-checks catch only the errors that differ between methods'. If every error were shared, interdependence would catch nothing, so the claim assumes that some errors differ between methods.

Why not (A): Catching errors requires that faulty methods sometimes disagree; if overlapping methods nearly always agreed, comparisons would rarely expose anything, so this works against the claim.

Why not (C): The argument does not need this: paragraph 2 contemplates 'A small mistake in the geometry of nearby stars', and paragraph 3 explains how errors anywhere can be caught.

Why not (D): Paragraph 4 leaves open whether the disagreement reveals 'a flaw common to the ladder's rungs' or 'something new about the cosmos', and the claim in paragraph 3 does not concern it.

Common misconception: Choosing an option that sounds like a precondition for a reliable ladder rather than one the specific claim requires.

CAT-VARC-P06-030 · Physics, cosmos and measurement · passage Medium · 450 words · 4 questions

Conservation laws are usually taught as the bedrock of physics. Energy is neither created nor destroyed; momentum lost by one body is gained by another; a spinning skater who draws in her arms turns faster because her angular momentum cannot change. Presented in this way, the laws look like the deepest facts we possess, discovered by patient measurement and accepted because nature has never been caught breaking them. A theorem proved in 1918 by the mathematician Emmy Noether suggests that this presentation, though not wrong, puts the foundations in the wrong place.

What Noether showed is that, for any system whose laws can be written in a certain standard mathematical form, every continuous symmetry of those laws corresponds to a quantity that is conserved. If the laws are the same today as they were yesterday, energy is conserved. If they are the same here as in the next room, momentum is conserved. If they do not depend on which way a system happens to be facing, angular momentum is conserved. The conservation laws, in other words, are not separate facts standing side by side; they are consequences of a single kind of fact, namely that the rules of nature do not care when, where or in what orientation they are applied.

It is tempting to conclude that conservation has thereby been proved, that energy must be conserved as a matter of logic. That would be a mistake. The theorem converts one question into another; it does not settle either. Whether the laws really are the same at all times is something only observation can tell us, and the theorem ensures that the evidence for the symmetry and the evidence for the conservation law are, in effect, the same evidence. What has changed is the direction of explanation. We no longer say that energy is conserved and leave the matter there; we say that energy is conserved because time is uniform, and we are then free to ask whether time is uniform.

That question has teeth. On the scale of the whole universe, which is expanding and so does not look the same from one epoch to the next, the symmetry behind energy conservation is not obviously present, and it is far from clear that a universal law of energy conservation can even be stated there. On the older view this would look like a scandal, a crack in the bedrock. On the view the theorem encourages, it is just what one should expect: where the symmetry fails, the conservation law loses its footing, and the surprise would be if it did not. A principle that once seemed beyond question has become something more useful, a claim whose limits can be foreseen.

CAT-VARC-P06-030-Q1 · Main idea / primary purpose · MediumMCQ
The passage is chiefly concerned with
  1. (A)arguing that conservation laws are better understood as consequences of symmetries, a shift that alters how they explain rather than proving them.
  2. (B)demonstrating that the conservation of energy is a truth of logic, which no observation of nature could ever overturn.
  3. (C)recounting how a mathematician's theorem persuaded physicists who had long doubted the conservation laws to accept them.
  4. (D)explaining how the expansion of the universe has forced physicists to give up the conservation of energy altogether.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 1 says the theorem 'puts the foundations in the wrong place'; paragraph 2 shows conservation laws following from symmetries; paragraph 3 says the change is in 'the direction of explanation', not a proof; paragraph 4 shows the payoff. The correct answer covers both the reframing and its limit.

Why not (B): Paragraph 3 calls this conclusion 'a mistake' and insists that only observation can tell us whether the underlying symmetry holds.

Why not (C): No earlier doubt about the conservation laws is reported; paragraph 1 says they were taught as 'bedrock' and the theorem relocates rather than restores them.

Why not (D): The expanding universe appears only in paragraph 4 as an application, and the passage says merely that a universal law is unclear 'there', not that it has been abandoned.

Common misconception: Mistaking the application in paragraph 4 for the subject, or overreading the theorem as a proof.

Difficulty: One wrong option states the very conclusion the author rejects, in confident terms; the correct answer must hold both halves of the argument. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

Why not (B): Paragraph 2 gives this pairing in so many words: 'If they are the same here as in the next room, momentum is conserved.'

CAT-VARC-P06-030-Q3 · Inference · MediumMCQ

  1. (A)count as evidence against the strict conservation of energy, since evidence about the symmetry is in effect evidence about conservation.
  2. (B)leave the conservation of energy untouched, since the theorem establishes conservation as a matter of logic alone.

Paragraph 3 says the theorem lets us 'ask whether time is uniform'; paragraph 4 opens 'That question has teeth' and shows the answer bearing on the expanding universe. Its final sentence states the function: the principle has become 'a claim whose limits can be foreseen'.
