CAT-VARC-P06-031 · Physics, cosmos and measurement · passage Difficult · 619 words · 5 questions

Quantum theory is among the most precisely confirmed bodies of physics ever constructed, and it is also, by the admission of many who use it, a theory that does not quite say what it is about. The difficulty is usually called the measurement problem. The theory's central equation describes a system by a mathematical object, the wave function, which evolves smoothly and predictably for as long as nobody looks. Under that evolution a particle can come to be in a combination, or superposition, of possibilities: here and there, spinning one way and the other. Yet whenever an experimenter checks, the apparatus records exactly one result. The textbook rule bridges the gap by decree: at the moment of measurement the wave function 'collapses' to one of the possibilities, with probabilities that the theory supplies.

The trouble is that the rule invokes a notion the theory never defines. A measuring device is made of atoms, and atoms obey the smooth equation; treated as part of the physical system, the device should itself enter a superposition of having recorded each result, rather than recording one. Somewhere between the particle and the experimenter's notebook the smooth evolution is supposed to give way to the abrupt one, but the theory does not say where, or why, or what counts as a measurement in the first place. In practice nothing hangs on the answer, because the boundary can be drawn anywhere convenient without altering the predicted statistics. That is precisely what makes the problem so easy to ignore.

The proposed solutions divide, roughly, by what they are prepared to give up. Some modify the smooth equation, adding a tiny spontaneous tendency to collapse that is negligible for single particles but overwhelming for anything as large as a pointer. Others leave the equation untouched and deny that collapse ever happens: every possible outcome is realised, each in a branch of reality that cannot interact with the others, and the appearance of a single result is simply what being in one branch is like. Still others add a further ingredient to the wave function, definite positions for particles at all times, guided by the wave, so that the outcome was settled all along. A widely discussed physical process, in which a system's entanglement with its surroundings rapidly suppresses the signs of superposition, is often offered as a solution in its own right; it explains why superpositions of large objects are never seen, but on most accounts it does not explain why one outcome occurs rather than another.

Because these rival pictures agree, in nearly every experiment so far performed, with each other and with the textbook rule, a common response is to call the dispute metaphysics, a matter of taste for people who have finished their calculations. The response is understandable but confused. It treats the textbook rule as a neutral baseline from which the interpretations depart, when the rule is itself an interpretation, and an incomplete one, since it relies on a word it cannot define. Nor are the rivals strictly equivalent: the theories that modify the equation predict small departures from standard behaviour in sufficiently large systems, and experiments of increasing delicacy are now searching for them.

None of this obliges the working physicist to choose a side before computing a reaction rate; the theory's practical success is real, and it does not wait on the outcome of the debate. But a theory that works without our knowing what it describes has not thereby been shown to need no description. The measurement problem is not a puzzle from philosophy grafted onto physics. It is a gap in the statement of the physics itself, one that the theory's very success has made easy to step around.

CAT-VARC-P06-031-Q1 · Structure / function · MediumMCQ
The observation in paragraph 2 that the boundary 'can be drawn anywhere convenient without altering the predicted statistics' serves mainly to
  1. (A)suggest that measuring devices, unlike the particles they record, do not obey the smooth equation of the theory.
  2. (B)show that the measurement problem has in effect been solved, since the location of the boundary makes no difference.
  3. (C)explain why a gap in the theory's foundations makes so little difference to how the theory is used.
  4. (D)support the claim that rival interpretations differ in their predictions only for sufficiently large systems.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 2 sets out the gap (the theory does not say where measurement happens) and then notes that 'In practice nothing hangs on the answer', because the boundary can be moved without changing predictions. The following sentence draws the consequence: that 'is precisely what makes the problem so easy to ignore'.

Why not (A): Paragraph 2 says the opposite: a device 'is made of atoms, and atoms obey the smooth equation'.

Why not (B): The next sentence says this is 'precisely what makes the problem so easy to ignore', and paragraph 5 calls it 'a gap in the statement of the physics itself', so the problem is not solved.

Why not (D): That claim, made in paragraph 4, concerns theories that modify the equation; paragraph 2 is about the textbook rule's undefined boundary.

Common misconception: Taking practical irrelevance as a solution, the confusion the author is arguing against.

Difficulty: The function is signalled by the sentence that follows, but one wrong option turns it into the dismissive view the passage rejects. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P06-032 · Physics, cosmos and measurement · passage Difficult · 584 words · 5 questions

Physicists have long remarked that several of the numbers fixing the behaviour of matter seem oddly well suited to the existence of anything as complicated as a living thing. Were certain forces a little stronger or a little weaker, the argument runs, stars would burn out too quickly or never ignite, atoms heavier than hydrogen would be scarce, and chemistry would offer nothing as intricate as a cell. How tightly these numbers are really constrained is debated, and some claims of extreme precision have not survived scrutiny. Enough of the pattern has held up, however, to pose a question: why should the constants of nature fall within the narrow ranges that permit observers to exist at all?

One family of answers appeals to what is usually called anthropic reasoning. Its modest form makes a point that sounds almost too obvious to state: whatever we observe must be compatible with our being there to observe it. We should not be astonished to find ourselves on a planet with liquid water and a tolerable climate, since observers could hardly have arisen anywhere else; to treat our surroundings as a fair sample of the cosmos would be like concluding that a town's residents are unusually fit because everyone one has met there was met at the swimming pool. In this form the principle is not a theory of anything. It is a warning about bias, and as a warning it is both correct and useful.

The difficulty begins when the same reasoning is asked to explain the constants themselves. Consider a lottery. A winner who learns that millions of tickets were sold need suspect nothing: someone had to win, and the winner is simply whoever did. But if only one ticket was ever printed and it won, the winner's existence explains nothing about why that ticket was drawn; it guarantees only that, had it not been, there would be no winner left to wonder. A selection effect does explanatory work only when there are many draws among which selection can operate. Applied to the cosmos, anthropic reasoning dissolves the puzzle of fine tuning only if there are many universes, or many regions of one, in which the constants take different values, and we necessarily find ourselves in one of the hospitable few.

Defenders of the approach reply that such an ensemble is not invented for the purpose. Some cosmological theories, developed to address quite different problems, appear to imply a vast number of regions with differing properties, and if those theories are right, the anthropic explanation comes, so to speak, free of charge. The reply has force, but it shifts the burden rather than lifting it. The explanation is now exactly as good as the theory that supplies the ensemble, and since the other regions are, on most versions, unobservable in principle, the evidence for that theory must come from somewhere else. Anthropic reasoning cannot certify its own premise.

None of this shows that the approach is unscientific, a charge often levelled against it with more heat than precision. Selection effects are a respectable part of reasoning in every empirical science, and ignoring them produces errors as surely as overrating them does. The narrower lesson is that the modest principle and the ambitious one are different claims and should not borrow each other's credibility. The first is nearly beyond dispute and explains almost nothing on its own; the second would explain a great deal, but only on a condition that the principle itself is powerless to establish.

CAT-VARC-P06-032-Q1 · Structure / function · EasyMCQ

  1. (A)how conclusions drawn from a sample selected by the conditions of observation can mislead.

  3. (C)why living things are more likely to arise on some planets than on others in the same system.
  4. (D)how the ambitious form of anthropic reasoning accounts for the fine tuning of physical constants.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 2 says treating our surroundings 'as a fair sample of the cosmos' would be like judging a town's fitness from people met at the pool, and concludes that the principle is 'a warning about bias'.

Why not (B): That claim belongs to paragraph 1; the pool comparison illustrates the 'warning about bias' of paragraph 2.

Why not (C): The comparison is about a biased sample of residents, not about where life arises; the planet remark only sets up the point about bias.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 concerns only the 'modest form'; the attempt to explain the constants begins in paragraph 3.

Common misconception: Linking the comparison to the fine tuning of the constants rather than to the modest principle.

Difficulty: The function is named in the sentences around the comparison. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P06-032-Q2 · Inference · MediumMCQ
It can be inferred from the lottery example that, in the author's view, the existence of observers would explain nothing about the constants if
  1. (A)ours were the only universe there is, with its constants fixed once and for all.
  2. (B)the other regions of a large ensemble turned out to be unobservable to us in principle.
  3. (C)a great many universes existed whose constants differed from ours, most of them hostile to life.
  4. (D)the constants permitted observers to exist only within some very narrow range of values.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 3: with a single ticket, the winner's existence 'explains nothing about why that ticket was drawn', and a selection effect 'does explanatory work only when there are many draws'. A single universe is the one-ticket case.

Why not (C): This is the case in which, according to paragraph 3, anthropic reasoning 'dissolves the puzzle'; it corresponds to millions of tickets sold.

Why not (D): That narrowness is the puzzle itself, set out in paragraph 1; it is not the condition under which the selection effect fails to explain.

Common misconception: Confusing a lack of evidence for an ensemble (paragraph 4) with the absence of an ensemble (paragraph 3).

Difficulty: Requires mapping the analogy onto the cosmic case and keeping apart two distinct difficulties the passage raises. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

Difficulty: Each wrong option matches part of the argument but distorts one element of it: the condition, the reality of fine tuning, or the value of the modest principle. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P06-033 · Medicine and the body · passage Easy · 438 words · 4 questions

Public health suffers from a peculiar handicap: when it succeeds, nothing happens. A hospital can display the patients it has cured and a surgeon can point to the tumour she removed, but the officials who ran a vaccination campaign have nothing to show for their work except an absence. The measles outbreak that did not occur leaves no photographs, no grateful survivors and no line in the local newspaper. Success in prevention is a count of events that never took place, and such a count is always an estimate rather than an observation.

This invisibility is more than an inconvenience for those who would like credit. It changes how the public weighs risk. When a disease has been made rare, the vaccine against it becomes more noticeable than the disease itself. A parent who has never seen a child struggle to breathe with diphtheria, but who has heard about a neighbour's child running a fever after an injection, is weighing something vivid against something abstract. From where that parent stands, the calculation is not foolish; the danger really has receded from ordinary experience. What the parent may not see is that it receded because of the very programme now being doubted, and that it can return if enough people reach the same conclusion.

The same pattern appears wherever the benefit of a measure is an event that fails to happen. A food inspection service that keeps contaminated products off the shelves is easy to cut in a lean year, because the year after the cut looks much like the year before it. The costs of neglect arrive slowly, are spread thinly, and are usually blamed on something else by the time they become visible. Spending on treatment, by contrast, answers a need that is present and named. It is not surprising that budgets drift towards cure and away from prevention even when prevention is the cheaper way to buy the same health.

None of this means that the public should simply take officials at their word. Claims about disasters averted are claims about a world that did not happen, and they can be inflated by any agency that wants to protect its budget. The answer to invisibility is not deference but better ways of making the counterfactual visible: comparisons with places and periods that lacked the measure, honest accounting of the measure's own costs and side effects, and records kept carefully enough that a sceptic could check them. Prevention will never be as photogenic as rescue, but it can be made legible, and a public that can read its record is more likely to keep paying for it.

  3. (C)governments spend too much on hospitals and should move that money to food inspection, where it would buy more health.

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 1 states the problem (success in prevention is 'an absence'), paragraphs 2 and 3 show how this invisibility distorts parents' judgements and budgets, and paragraph 4 proposes making the record 'legible' rather than demanding trust. The correct answer captures both the problem and the author's remedy.

Why not (A): Paragraph 4 says the public should not 'simply take officials at their word' and warns that such claims 'can be inflated', so the passage asks for checkable records, not more trust.

Why not (C): Food inspection is one illustration in paragraph 3, and the passage makes no recommendation to transfer hospital budgets; the option is too narrow and adds a prescription.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 says the vaccine becomes more 'noticeable' than the disease, not more dangerous; the option turns a point about perception into a claim about actual risk.

Common misconception: Taking one illustration (food inspection budgets) for the whole argument, or missing the author's refusal to endorse deference.

Difficulty: The structure is signposted and each wrong option conflicts with an explicit sentence. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P06-034 · Medicine and the body · passage Easy · 411 words · 4 questions

Few health instructions are repeated as confidently as the one about sleep: adults need about eight hours, and those hours should come in a single unbroken block. Someone who wakes at three in the morning and lies awake for an hour is likely to describe the experience as a disorder, and to spend the next day worrying about it. Advice is sought, remedies are bought, and an hour of wakefulness becomes a problem to be solved. Yet the second half of that instruction, the part about continuity, has a shorter history than its confidence suggests.

Historians reading diaries, legal testimony and household advice from pre-industrial Europe have noticed casual references to a 'first sleep' and a 'second sleep', separated by a spell of wakefulness in the middle of the night. People used that interval to pray, tend the fire, talk quietly, or simply lie in the dark and think. What is striking about these references is not their content but their tone: the waking interval is mentioned as an ordinary feature of the night, not as a complaint. One explanation offered for its later disappearance is the spread of cheap artificial light, which pushed bedtimes later and compressed the night into a single shorter stretch, though the evidence for this is suggestive rather than conclusive.

If consolidated sleep is partly a product of custom, then some of what is now labelled insomnia may be a mismatch between a person and a norm rather than a fault in the person. This matters because worry about sleep is itself a powerful disrupter of sleep. A person who wakes in the night and concludes that something has gone wrong becomes more alert, and stays awake longer, than one who regards the waking as unremarkable. The norm, in other words, can help produce the very failures it is used to diagnose.

It would be a mistake, however, to replace one myth with another. The historical record does not show that segmented sleep is the true human pattern from which industrial society has led us astray; it shows that sleep has taken more than one form, depending on light, work and season. Nor does any of this make chronic sleep loss harmless. People who sleep too little over long periods do suffer for it, and some sleep complaints call for medical attention. The modest lesson is that a description of how many people sleep should not be mistaken for a rule about how everyone must.

CAT-VARC-P06-035 · Medicine and the body · passage Medium · 418 words · 4 questions

In the design of a clinical trial, the placebo plays the role of nothing. Patients in one group receive the drug, patients in the other receive an inert imitation, and whatever improvement the second group shows is subtracted from the first as background noise. The logic is sound for its purpose, which is to establish whether a molecule does anything beyond the act of being given. But the habit of treating the placebo arm as a measure of nothing has encouraged a further and less defensible assumption, that the improvement seen there is not real, or at least not interesting.

It is both. What patients in a placebo group respond to is not the sugar in the pill but everything that surrounds it: the consultation, the explanation, the expectation of relief, the ritual of taking something at set hours, the attention of people whose job is to help. Some researchers prefer to speak of a 'meaning response' for this reason, since the inert pill is merely the occasion on which the meaning of being treated is delivered. On this view the placebo arm is not a blank but a measurement of how much the context of care can do by itself.

The standard objection to using such effects deliberately has been ethical. A placebo, it was assumed, works only if the patient is deceived, and deceiving patients violates the trust on which medicine depends. Several small trials have unsettled this assumption by giving patients pills that were openly described as inert and still recording improvements in symptoms that the patients reported themselves. The findings are preliminary, and it is not clear how much of the effect survives once the novelty of an unusual study wears off. Even so, they suggest that deception may be less essential to the response than was believed, and that the ritual may matter more than the belief that the pill is active.

Enthusiasts are quick to go further than the evidence allows. Responses to placebo are most consistent for symptoms that are experienced and reported, such as pain, nausea and fatigue; there is little reason to think that the meaning of a consultation can shrink a tumour or clear an infection. The more useful conclusion is not that doctors should prescribe sugar pills but that the manner of care is itself a part of treatment. A health system that shortens consultations to save money may be removing an active ingredient without ever recording the loss, because its effect was always filed under nothing.

CAT-VARC-P06-036 · Medicine and the body · passage Medium · 415 words · 4 questions

Discussions of antibiotic resistance usually begin with misuse: drugs prescribed for viral infections that they cannot touch, courses abandoned halfway, animals dosed to make them grow faster. Each of these practices gives bacteria more opportunities to evolve defences, and each deserves the attention it receives. Less often discussed is a quieter problem that has nothing to do with carelessness. Even if every prescription were perfectly judged, the way new antibiotics are paid for would discourage anyone from making them.

Consider what responsible use of a genuinely new antibiotic would look like. Because every use exerts selective pressure, the new drug should be held back for patients whose infections resist older and cheaper treatments, and it should be prescribed as rarely as possible for as long as possible. This is sound medicine. It is also a commercial disaster. A company recovers the cost of developing a drug by selling it in quantity during the years its patent protects it, and a drug that doctors are urged not to use cannot be sold in quantity. The contrast with a medicine for a chronic condition, taken every day for decades by millions of people, could hardly be sharper. It is not surprising that large firms have drifted out of the field, or that small firms that have brought new antibiotics through approval have sometimes failed commercially soon afterwards.

The proposed remedy is to separate a company's income from the number of doses it sells. Under such schemes, a health system would pay a fixed annual sum for access to a new antibiotic, whether it is used on a thousand patients or on ten, much as a town pays for a fire brigade whether or not anything burns that year. The payment rewards the existence of the drug rather than its consumption, which is exactly the property a reserve antibiotic is supposed to have.

The objections are real. Such payments are large, they must be justified to budget holders who see little use for their money, and nobody knows quite how to price a drug whose value lies mainly in emergencies it may never have to meet. Nor would better payment by itself guarantee discovery, since finding compounds that kill resistant bacteria without harming patients is scientifically hard. But these difficulties argue for designing the schemes carefully, not for abandoning them. The alternative is to go on rewarding a pattern of sales that good medicine forbids, and to discover the cost only when the older drugs have stopped working.

Answer: (C)

CAT-VARC-P06-036-Q4 · Strengthen / weaken / assumption · DifficultMCQ
The argument in paragraph 2 that responsible use makes a new antibiotic 'a commercial disaster' depends most on which of the following assumptions?
  1. (A)Older and cheaper antibiotics will soon stop working against most common infections, leaving doctors with no alternative to the new drug.
  2. (B)The price per course of a reserve antibiotic cannot be set high enough to recover its costs from a few patients.
  3. (C)Medicines for chronic conditions cost more to develop than new antibiotics do, which gives their makers stronger incentives.
  4. (D)A genuinely new antibiotic will usually prove less effective against common infections than the older and cheaper drugs it backs up.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

The step from 'cannot be sold in quantity' to 'commercial disaster' holds only if low volume cannot be offset by a high price per course. If a company could charge enough per course to recover its costs from a small number of patients, responsible use would not be ruinous. That unstated premise is what the correct answer states.

Why not (A): If older drugs failed widely, the new drug would be used far more, which would ease the commercial problem rather than underlie it; the argument assumes older drugs still work for most patients.

Why not (C): The contrast with chronic medicines turns on how often they are taken, not on what they cost to develop; the argument works whatever the relative costs.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 holds the new drug back because 'every use exerts selective pressure', not because it works less well; the commercial problem arises however effective the drug is.

Common misconception: Choosing the option about older drugs failing, which sounds like a premise of any argument about resistance but would in fact ease the commercial problem.

Difficulty: The needed premise is unstated, and two wrong options sound like natural premises of an argument about resistance and drug development. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P06-037 · Medicine and the body · passage Medium · 411 words · 4 questions

For most of the twentieth century, medicine described its relationship with microbes in the language of war. Bacteria invaded, the immune system defended, and antibiotics were weapons that wiped out the enemy. The metaphor was not idle decoration. It organised research around identifying a single culprit for each disease and finding a means of killing it, and that programme saved an enormous number of lives.

The study of the microbes that live in and on healthy bodies has made the war metaphor look crude. Most of these organisms are not enemies; many are useful, helping to digest food, train the immune system and occupy space that harmful species would otherwise take. An ecological vocabulary has therefore become popular: the body is a habitat, the gut is a crowded ecosystem, and health is a matter of the right community rather than the absence of germs. This vocabulary explains things the older one could not. It makes sense of why a broad course of antibiotics can be followed by a stubborn intestinal infection: by clearing out the resident species, the drug removes the competitors that had kept a harmful one in check. It also makes sense of why transferring gut bacteria from a healthy donor has proved effective against that very infection.

Yet the new metaphor carries risks of its own, and they are less often noticed because it sounds so benign. Ecology, popularly understood, suggests balance and harmony, as though every body had a single proper community to which it should be restored. Ecologists themselves are more cautious; real ecosystems have many stable states, change with season and disturbance, and do not come with a correct composition. The popular version has proved ideal for marketing. Products that promise to 'rebalance' the gut can borrow the authority of a serious science while specifying nothing that could be measured, and the vagueness of the promise protects it from ever being shown false.

The lesson is not that medicine should return to the language of war, nor that metaphors can be avoided. Every science thinks partly through images, and the choice is between images that sharpen questions and images that blur them. The ecological picture is valuable when it brings with it the working concepts of ecology, such as competition, succession and the effects of disturbance, which generate predictions that can be tested. It becomes a liability when it brings only the mood of ecology, a feeling of harmony that no experiment could ever contradict.

CAT-VARC-P06-038 · Medicine and the body · passage Medium · 408 words · 4 questions

The ordinary picture of pain is that of a gauge. Tissue is damaged, nerves carry a report of the damage to the brain, and the strength of the pain reflects the size of the injury, much as the needle on a dial reflects the pressure in a pipe. The picture is intuitive and, for a great many everyday injuries, roughly accurate. A burnt finger hurts more than a scratched one, and both stop hurting as they heal.

The difficulty is that the gauge picture fails precisely where pain matters most. People sometimes suffer serious wounds in moments of danger or intense concentration and feel little until the moment has passed. Others feel vivid pain in limbs that have been amputated, where there is no tissue left to report anything. Most troubling of all, a large number of people continue to hurt for months or years after an injury has, by every available measure, healed. If pain were a reading of damage, none of this should happen. A pressure gauge that behaved in these ways would simply be sent back for repair.

An alternative account treats pain not as a reading but as a verdict. Signals from the tissues are one input among several; the brain combines them with context, expectation and memory, and produces pain when it concludes that the body is under threat and needs protecting. On this account, the soldier who feels nothing has a brain occupied with a greater threat, the amputee's brain is responding to a map of a body part that persists after the part is gone, and long-lasting pain may reflect a protective system that has grown oversensitive, raising the alarm long after the danger has passed.

This account is sometimes heard as a polite way of saying that the pain is imaginary, and patients with long-standing pain have good reason to be wary of anything that sounds like that. But the claim is the reverse. A verdict produced by the nervous system is as real as any other bodily event, and it hurts just as much. What changes is where treatment might be aimed. If the alarm itself has become oversensitive, then searching ever harder for a damaged structure, and operating on whatever turns up in a scan, may do little good, while approaches that address the protective system as a whole, through explanation, graded activity and attention to fear, may help where repairs to the tissues did not.

CAT-VARC-P06-039 · Medicine and the body · passage Difficult · 592 words · 5 questions

Few medical ideas seem as self-evidently right as early detection. A disease found sooner is a disease treated sooner, and a disease treated sooner, surely, is more often cured. Screening programmes, which look for disease in people who feel perfectly well, are built on this reasoning, and the figures they produce appear to confirm it. People whose cancers are found by screening typically survive far longer after diagnosis than people whose cancers are found because they caused symptoms. Patients who have been through screening often say as much, and public campaigns repeat the message with understandable conviction. It is hard to imagine clearer evidence that looking early pays.

The trouble is that the comparison is rigged, though by the arithmetic of detection rather than by anyone's intent. Suppose a tumour will kill a woman at seventy whatever is done. If it causes symptoms at sixty-eight, she survives two years after diagnosis; if a scan finds it at sixty-three, she survives seven. Nothing about her death has changed, but the survival figure has more than tripled, simply because the clock was started earlier. A second distortion arises from the different speeds at which tumours grow. A test repeated every few years is far more likely to catch a slow tumour, which lingers in a detectable but harmless state for a long time, than a fast one, which may appear and cause symptoms between two tests. Screened groups are therefore loaded with the gentler cases, and their better survival partly reflects what was found rather than what was done.

The most uncomfortable distortion takes this tendency to its limit. Some of the abnormalities found by screening, though they look like cancer under the microscope, would never have grown enough to cause harm in the person's lifetime. Detecting them is called overdiagnosis, and it is not an error in the usual sense, since the cells are what the pathologist says they are. The error lies in the prediction that they would have mattered, and since such a lesion is removed or destroyed once it is found, the prediction is never put to the test. People with such findings are treated, sometimes with surgery or radiation, and they then appear in the statistics as survivors, which of course they are, though they were never in danger. Because no individual can know that his or her own tumour was of this harmless kind, every overdiagnosed patient experiences the treatment as a rescue. Screening thus produces its own most sincere advocates, and the more it overdiagnoses, the more grateful survivors it creates.

None of this shows that screening is useless. For some cancers, carefully conducted trials comparing large screened and unscreened populations have found fewer deaths among those screened, and those programmes deserve their place. The point concerns the evidence, not the verdict. The question to ask of a screening programme is not how long its patients live after diagnosis but whether fewer people in the population it serves die of the disease, and whether that benefit outweighs the harm of treating people who were never going to be ill. Survival after diagnosis cannot answer that question, because it improves under the influence of lead time, slow tumours and overdiagnosis even when not a single death has been prevented. Doctors are not immune to the appeal of the survival figure, since it describes the patients they actually see; the deaths a programme fails to prevent, and the needless treatments it imposes, are visible only in comparisons between whole populations that no single clinic can make.

CAT-VARC-P06-039-Q4 · Strengthen / weaken / assumption · DifficultMCQ

  2. (B)People who chose to attend the programme were less likely to die of the disease than people in the same area who chose not to attend.
  3. (C)When comparable populations were assigned at random to be screened or not, fewer people in the screened population died of the disease.

Why not (A): This is the survival comparison that paragraph 4 says 'cannot answer that question', because lead time, slow tumours and overdiagnosis inflate it even when no death is prevented.

Why not (B): The author asks whether fewer people 'in the population it serves' die, a question answered only by 'comparisons between whole populations'; attenders and non-attenders are self-selected parts of one population, so the gap may reflect who chooses to attend rather than what screening does.

Why not (D): More and earlier diagnoses are exactly what overdiagnosis and the bias towards slow tumours would produce, so this finding cannot distinguish benefit from distortion.

Common misconception: Choosing the attenders comparison because it measures deaths, without noticing that it compares self-selected groups rather than whole populations.

Difficulty: Two wrong options reflect the distortions the passage describes, and the third measures mortality but in the wrong comparison, so the reader must apply the full criterion. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P06-040 · Medicine and the body · passage Difficult · 573 words · 5 questions

A randomised trial, for all its authority, answers a question about groups. It tells us that among patients like those enrolled, the people given a treatment did better on average than the people who were not, by an amount that can be estimated with known uncertainty. The doctor in a consulting room faces a different question, about a single person, and the gap between the two questions has produced two opposite errors, each of which presents itself as wisdom.

The first error is to apply the average as though it were a fact about each patient. If a drug lowers the risk of a stroke over five years from ten in a hundred to seven in a hundred, it is tempting to say that it reduces every patient's risk by three points. But the trial cannot show this. The same average would result if a small minority benefited a great deal and everyone else not at all, or if many benefited modestly while a few were harmed. An average is a summary of a distribution, and very different distributions share the same summary. Even afterwards the matter stays hidden: a patient who takes the drug and has no stroke cannot tell whether the drug or good fortune spared him. To promise a particular patient a reduction of three points is to claim knowledge the trial does not contain.

The second error starts from this observation and draws the wrong conclusion. Since every patient is different, it says, trial results do not apply to the person in front of me, and I must rely on my clinical judgement. The premise is true and the conclusion does not follow. That patients differ is not news; what matters is whether they differ in ways that change how they respond to this treatment, and the clinician's impression is a poor instrument for discovering that. A doctor's experience is itself a kind of trial, but one without randomisation, with a small sample, no comparison group and a memory that favours vivid cases. If a large trial cannot reveal who benefits, a recollection of a few dozen patients is unlikely to do better.

Nor is the obvious remedy, examining subgroups within the trial, as reliable as it appears. Divide a trial's patients by age, sex, severity and a dozen other features, and some subgroups will show striking benefit or none at all purely by chance; the more divisions are made, the more such mirages appear. Findings of this kind have often failed to reappear when tested again. This does not mean that genuine differences in response do not exist. It means that claims about them need evidence as demanding as the evidence for the average itself, such as a plausible mechanism together with a difference that recurs in a fresh trial.

What follows is neither the tyranny of the average nor the licence of intuition. The average is the best default available when nothing more is known, and departures from it should be justified by reasons rather than by a sense that this patient is special. For some chronic conditions whose symptoms come and go quickly, it is even possible to run a trial within a single patient, alternating the treatment with a placebo over several periods and comparing the results. Such designs are not always practical, but they show what an answer to the individual question would actually require, and how little the bare assertion of individuality provides.

CAT-VARC-P06-041 · Computing, AI and data · passage Easy · 441 words · 4 questions

For most of its history the word 'computer' named an occupation rather than a machine. Observatories, survey offices and, later, engineering firms employed rooms of people whose work consisted of carrying out long calculations, often for tables of positions, tides or trajectories that others would use. It is tempting to treat these workers as a quaint prelude to the real story, a stopgap that lasted only until electronics arrived. That reading gets the relation backwards. The electronic computer did not introduce the idea of mechanical calculation into a world that lacked it; it inherited an idea that human computing offices had already worked out in detail.

The key feature of such offices was not the arithmetic skill of any individual but the way the work was organised. A planner with mathematical training would break a difficult problem into a sequence of simple steps, write those steps on printed sheets, and hand the sheets to workers who needed to know only how to add, subtract and look up values in a table. The workers did not have to understand why a step was there, and the scheme was in fact designed so that they would not need to. Errors were caught not by insight but by procedure: two people would compute the same column independently, and any disagreement sent the column back. What was being built, in effect, was a method in which correctness no longer depended on anyone grasping the whole.

Seen this way, much of what we think of as distinctive about programming was already present. The separation between the person who designs a procedure and the agent who executes it, the reduction of judgement to rules, the checking of results by redundancy rather than by understanding: all of these were practices before they were properties of machines. When the first electronic machines were built, their designers could borrow not only mathematical techniques from the computing offices but also a whole conception of what it meant to make a calculation reliable.

None of this is to say that the machines merely did faster what people had done slowly. Speed of the magnitude electronics provided changed which problems were worth posing at all; calculations that would have occupied a room of workers for years became routine, and whole fields grew up around them. But the continuity matters, because it reminds us that the habit of treating thought as something that can be divided, written down and handed to an executor who need not understand it is a social invention before it is a technical one. Its consequences, good and bad, were visible in offices long before they were visible on screens.

CAT-VARC-P06-042 · Computing, AI and data · passage Medium · 437 words · 4 questions

The most familiar complaint about recommendation algorithms is that they trap each of us in a private enclosure. Because a system learns what a user has clicked, watched or bought, the argument runs, it will keep offering more of the same, until every listener hears only the music that confirms their existing taste and every reader meets only the opinions they already hold. On this picture the danger is fragmentation: a public that once shared a common stock of songs, books and news splinters into millions of sealed niches, each tuned to one person.

The complaint is not baseless, but it may have the main tendency of these systems the wrong way round. Most recommendation engines do not learn from one user in isolation; they learn from the aggregate behaviour of very large numbers of users, and they rank items partly by how much engagement those items have already attracted. That design creates a feedback loop. An item that gets a slight early lead is shown to more people, which earns it more engagement, which earns it still more prominence. Imagine two songs of equal appeal released on the same day, one of which happens to be picked up by a few enthusiastic listeners in its first hours. Under such a loop the small, accidental difference can harden into an enormous gap, and the second song may effectively disappear, not because anyone judged it worse but because no one was shown it.

If this is right, the characteristic risk is less fragmentation than concentration: attention pooling ever more heavily on a few items, while the vast catalogue that digital distribution made available sits largely unvisited. The early hope that online platforms would let obscure work find its small audiences depended on people actually encountering that work, and a ranking system that rewards prior popularity makes such encounters rarer than the size of the catalogue suggests.

It would be wrong to present this as something algorithms invented. Bestseller lists, radio playlists and the front tables of bookshops have always amplified what was already selling, and cultural markets have long been marked by a few large successes and many neglected works. What is new is the speed of the loop and its invisibility. A bookseller choosing what to display is a person whose choices can be questioned; a ranking produced by continuous automated feedback appears simply to report what people want, when it is partly manufacturing it. The remedy, if there is one, lies less in protecting users from their own tastes than in asking how much of what looks like popular demand is an echo of earlier exposure.

Common misconception: Confusing the example with the fragmentation view it is meant to correct.

CAT-VARC-P06-043 · Computing, AI and data · passage Medium · 426 words · 4 questions

Debates about privacy in the age of large data sets usually begin from an image of ownership. Each person, on this view, holds a store of information about themselves, and the moral question is whether that information is taken with or without their permission. Hence the enormous weight placed on consent: the checkbox, the privacy notice, the setting that lets a user decline to share. If people are told what will happen to their data and agree to it, the transaction is treated as legitimate, and if they refuse, they are thought to have protected themselves.

The difficulty is that much personal information is not individual in the way the image assumes. A person who uploads their contact list discloses the names and numbers of people who agreed to nothing. Someone who submits a sample to a genetic testing service reveals facts about parents, siblings and cousins, some of whom may not know the test was taken. More subtly, when enough people share information about their habits, a system can learn the patterns that link one trait to another, and then infer those traits in people who shared nothing at all. A person who declines to disclose, say, a health condition may find that it has been estimated, with fair accuracy, from purchases and movements that resemble those of people who did disclose it.

One might reply that the refuser has still kept their own data, and that what others choose to reveal is their own affair. But this misses what the refuser was trying to protect. Nobody declines to share a fact for the sake of a particular row in a database; they decline because they do not want the fact known. If it can be known anyway, through the choices of others, then the refusal has secured very little, and the consent framework, which places the whole burden of protection on individual decisions, has quietly ceased to describe what is actually happening.

This does not make individual consent worthless. Being asked still matters, and a person who is deceived about how their information will be used has been wronged in a way that the collective character of data does not erase. The point is that consent cannot carry the load it has been given. Privacy behaves in part like clean air or quiet streets: a condition that each person's conduct affects for others, and that no one can secure for themselves by private choice alone. Protecting it therefore requires rules about what may be inferred and done, not merely about who has clicked agree.

CAT-VARC-P06-043-Q1 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ
According to the passage, a person who submits a sample to a genetic testing service
  1. (A)gives up legal ownership of the sample and of any information that may later be drawn from it.

CAT-VARC-P06-044 · Computing, AI and data · passage Easy · 412 words · 4 questions

When an automated system fails and a human operator fails to rescue it, the official account usually ends with the words 'human error'. The phrase is accurate in a narrow sense, since a person did do the wrong thing, but it hides the way the situation was produced. Automation is typically introduced to take over the parts of a task that are routine and well understood. What remains for the human is whatever the designers could not automate: the rare, ambiguous, fast-moving situations in which the machine gives up or behaves strangely. The person is thus left with the hardest part of the job, and is expected to perform it suddenly, after long periods of doing almost nothing.

Those long periods matter. Skill at controlling a process is kept alive by exercising it, and an operator who has spent months watching a screen rather than flying, steering or adjusting will have less of it than one who did the work by hand. Worse, the automation removes the everyday experience through which people build an intuitive model of how the system behaves, so that when something goes wrong they must reason about it from first principles, under pressure, with alarms sounding. Monitoring is also something human beings do badly. Attention drifts when nothing happens for hours, and the more reliable the machine, the longer the stretches in which nothing happens.

The result is a curious inversion. The better the automation works in ordinary conditions, the less prepared its human supervisors may be for the extraordinary ones, and the more surprising their failure will look when it comes. Calling that failure human error locates the fault in the individual at the very moment when the design of the whole arrangement deserves scrutiny.

None of this is a case against automation, which has removed a great deal of tedium and many kinds of mistake. It is a case for designing automated systems around the people who must step in when they fail. That might mean having operators run the process manually at regular intervals so that their skill does not decay, building displays that show what the machine is doing and why rather than only whether it is working, or deciding deliberately that some tasks should remain shared even when they could be fully automated. Such measures cost efficiency in the short run. Their justification is that the moments they prepare for, although rare, are the ones in which the most is at stake.

Why not (C): Paragraph 4 says 'None of this is a case against automation', which has removed 'many kinds of mistake'.

Common misconception: Reading criticism of the 'human error' label as criticism of automation itself.

CAT-VARC-P06-045 · Computing, AI and data · passage Medium · 449 words · 4 questions

Few technical metaphors have succeeded as completely as the cloud. To store files 'in the cloud' is to imagine them floating somewhere overhead, available from anywhere, belonging to no place in particular and weighing nothing at all. The image is not simply wrong; from the point of view of a user, it describes the experience rather well. A photograph taken on a phone appears on a laptop moments later, and the user neither knows nor needs to know where it has been in between. Yet the metaphor has migrated from describing an experience to shaping how whole societies think about a vast industrial system, and in that second role it misleads.

What sits behind the cloud is emphatically material. It consists of large buildings filled with servers, which consume great quantities of electricity and must be kept cool, often with water drawn from local supplies; of cables laid across sea floors and along railway lines; of particular sites chosen for cheap power, favourable taxes or stable ground. Each of these is somewhere, and being somewhere means being subject to the laws of a particular state, to the weather and geology of a particular region, and to the politics of the towns asked to host the buildings and supply their power. A data centre can strain a local grid or compete with farms for water, and a damaged cable can slow communication across a region.

The metaphor makes all of this hard to see. A cloud has no address, so questions about which country's courts can demand access to stored data come to seem technical rather than political. A cloud uses nothing, so the energy cost of keeping old files indefinitely available is easy to forget. And a cloud is diffuse, which suggests resilience, whereas in practice a large share of the services people rely on may depend on a small number of companies and sites, so that a single failure can ripple outward with surprising speed.

Defenders of the metaphor might point out that hiding complexity is precisely what good computing does. Nobody wants to think about the location of a server before sending a message, and abstractions of this kind are why ordinary people can use powerful systems at all. That is true, but it confuses two audiences. Abstraction is a gift to the user at the moment of use; it becomes a liability when it is carried over into the deliberations of citizens, regulators and planners, whose task is precisely to decide where the buildings go, who supplies their power and whose laws govern what they hold. For them, the useful question is not where the cloud is but how it came to seem to be nowhere.

CAT-VARC-P06-046 · Computing, AI and data · passage Difficult · 451 words · 4 questions

Whenever a machine produces fluent language, the question returns of whether it understands what it says. Two answers dominate. One holds that understanding is shown in behaviour: if a system answers questions, follows instructions and sustains a conversation in ways we cannot distinguish from a competent person, there is nothing further we could reasonably ask of it, and to withhold the word is mere prejudice in favour of our own kind. The other holds that behaviour, however impressive, can be produced by manipulating symbols whose connection to the world the system never grasps, and that understanding requires something behind the performance, some grounding of words in perception, action or experience.

The two camps disagree about almost everything except one assumption, and that shared assumption is where the trouble lies. Both treat understanding as a single property that a system either has or lacks, so that the only task is to decide which side of the line it falls on. Yet when we attribute understanding to people we do nothing of the kind. A student may understand a theorem well enough to apply it to textbook problems but not well enough to see why it fails when one condition is dropped; a mechanic may understand an engine thoroughly in practice while being unable to explain its thermodynamics. We judge understanding by a battery of loosely connected abilities: explaining, extending to unfamiliar cases, noticing one's own mistakes, connecting what one knows to what one does. These abilities can come apart, and people possess them in very different measures.

If understanding is a cluster of this kind, the all-or-nothing question about machines is badly posed. A system may display some of the abilities to a high degree and others hardly at all; it may extend a pattern brilliantly in one domain and fail, without any sign of noticing, at a trivial variation in another. Saying that it 'really understands' flattens this profile into a verdict that tells us little, and saying that it 'merely manipulates symbols' does the same in the opposite direction. The useful work lies in mapping which abilities are present, under what conditions, and how robustly.

This proposal will not satisfy everyone. Someone who believes that understanding involves an inner experience of meaning will say that mapping abilities, however finely, leaves the essential question untouched. Perhaps it does. But that question, whatever its answer, is a question about experience, and it arises equally for any being whose inner life we cannot inspect. It should not be allowed to stand in for the more tractable question of what a system can and cannot reliably do, which is, after all, the question on which most practical decisions about such systems depend.

  4. (D)defend the view that behaviour indistinguishable from that of a competent person is sufficient evidence of genuine understanding.

Common misconception: Taking one of the reported views for the author's own.

CAT-VARC-P06-047 · Computing, AI and data · passage Difficult · 572 words · 5 questions

Suppose a lender uses a scoring system to estimate how likely each applicant is to default, and suppose applicants belong to two groups that differ, for whatever historical reasons, in how often their members in fact default. There are several natural ways to ask whether the system treats the groups fairly. One may ask whether a given score means the same thing in both groups, so that among applicants scored as having a one-in-five chance of default, roughly one in five defaults whichever group they belong to. One may ask instead whether applicants who would in fact have repaid are wrongly refused at the same rate in both groups, or whether those who would in fact have defaulted are wrongly approved at the same rate. Each of these looks like a reasonable requirement, and each has been proposed as the meaning of fairness.

It turns out that they cannot, in general, all be satisfied together. When the groups' underlying default rates differ and the prediction is less than perfect, a score that means the same thing in both groups will produce different rates of error between them, in wrongful refusals, wrongful approvals or both, and equalising both kinds of error will make the same score mean different things in different groups. This is not a defect of any particular system that better engineering might remove; it follows from the arithmetic of prediction under unequal base rates. The finding was greeted in some quarters as a kind of scandal, and two conclusions were quickly drawn from it: that the idea of a fair algorithm is incoherent, or, conversely, that since some criterion must be chosen, the choice is a technical matter best left to the people who build the systems.

Both conclusions seem to me mistaken, and for related reasons. The impossibility does not show that fairness is incoherent; it shows that our everyday notion of fairness bundles together several demands that usually travel together and part company only under specific conditions. Discovering that two values can conflict is not the same as discovering that one of them is empty, any more than the occasional conflict between honesty and kindness shows that one of these is an illusion. Nor does the need to choose make the choice technical. Which criterion should give way depends on what the decision does to the people it touches: who bears the cost of being wrongly refused, how severe that cost is, and whether the different base rates are themselves the residue of earlier unfairness that the decision would perpetuate. Those are questions about harm and responsibility, not about arithmetic.

It is worth adding that the dilemma is not peculiar to algorithms. A loan officer who judges applicants by experience faces exactly the same trade-off whenever the groups differ in base rates; the officer simply does not have to declare which criterion is being sacrificed, and may not know. In this respect the formal analysis has done something valuable. By making the conflict explicit, it has turned a choice that was always being made silently into one that must be made in the open, and has removed the comfortable belief that a sufficiently careful decision maker could satisfy every reasonable demand at once. The danger is that the explicitness is squandered, that the choice is buried again inside the system, this time under a layer of technical vocabulary instead of a layer of personal discretion.

CAT-VARC-P06-048 · Computing, AI and data · passage Medium · 616 words · 5 questions

Two complaints about digital information circulate side by side, and at first sight they cannot both be true. The first is that the internet never forgets: an embarrassing photograph or an ill-judged remark, once posted, may resurface years later, copied onto sites its author has never heard of. The second is that digital records are alarmingly fragile: web pages vanish, links lead nowhere, and files saved in formats that no current program can read become, for practical purposes, meaningless strings of bits. One might expect a medium to be either durable or perishable, and it is puzzling to hear the same medium accused of both.

The puzzle dissolves once we stop asking about the medium and start asking about copying. A single digital copy is, if anything, more perishable than a sheet of paper. Paper survives neglect; a letter left in a drawer can usually be read a century later by anyone who knows the language. A file left on an old disk depends on the disk surviving, on a machine that can still read the disk, and on software that can still interpret the file, and each of these dependencies decays on its own schedule. What digital information has, and paper largely lacks, is the capacity to be copied perfectly, instantly and at almost no cost. Its persistence therefore depends far less on the durability of any one copy than on whether enough people, or enough systems, have reasons to keep making copies.

Seen in this light, the two complaints describe the same mechanism working on different material. The embarrassing photograph persists because it is the kind of thing people want to pass on, so it is duplicated faster than any single copy can be deleted. The municipal report, the small society's newsletter and the scholar's personal website persist only as long as someone pays for the server and remembers to renew the domain, because few people have any reason to copy them. Nothing about these documents makes them harder to store than the photograph; a newsletter occupies less space than most images. What they lack is a constituency of copiers. When the society disbands or the scholar retires, the single copy that existed sits on a server whose bills no one is paying, and one day it is simply gone, often without anyone noticing that it has happened. Digital persistence, in short, tracks attention and interest rather than value, and the material most likely to vanish is often the unglamorous record that matters most to a historian a generation later.

This suggests that preservation in a digital age is less a technical problem than an institutional one. Better storage media help, but no medium keeps information alive by itself; what keeps it alive is an organisation that has made copying it someone's duty, and that will go on paying for that duty when the material has ceased to be interesting. Libraries and archives have long performed this role for print, and some are trying to perform it for the web. It is tempting to hope that automated tools, which can gather enormous quantities of web pages without human effort, have made such institutions unnecessary. They have not: what such tools gather must still be stored, checked and kept readable as formats change, and someone must decide what deserves that continuing expense. Archives face a difficulty that print archives faced less acutely: the sheer volume of material means that they must choose, and every choice about what to copy is also, in effect, a choice about what to let disappear. The task cannot be avoided by declaring that everything is saved, because what is left to the accidents of attention is saved very unevenly.

CAT-VARC-P06-049 · Science as an institution · passage Easy · 451 words · 4 questions

Few practices in science are defended as warmly, or examined as rarely, as anonymous peer review. A manuscript arrives at a journal, an editor sends it to two or three specialists, and their verdicts, delivered without signatures, decide whether the work enters the published record. The usual defence of the anonymity is straightforward and has real force. A young researcher asked to assess a paper by the most powerful figure in her field may hesitate to point out its flaws if her name is attached to the report, since that figure may one day sit on the committee that decides her promotion. Anonymity, on this view, is what allows candour to flow upward.

The trouble is that the same protection shelters other things as well. A referee who is quietly working on a rival theory, or whose own earlier results are contradicted by the manuscript, can recommend rejection without ever having to explain himself in public. Most referees, of course, do nothing of the kind; the difficulty is that nobody outside the editorial office can tell which ones do. Authors receive a verdict but not a reason they can contest, and readers never learn that a paper was turned away at all. A system designed to free the weak from the strong can thus end up freeing the established from scrutiny, because it is usually the established who are asked to referee, and a manuscript that challenges a reigning view is, by the nature of the case, most likely to be sent to the very people who hold that view.

It would nevertheless be wrong to infer from all this that anonymity is, in itself, the problem. What makes it dangerous is its combination with gatekeeping power. When a referee's report merely advises, when it is published alongside the paper as one reader's criticism that the authors can answer, the identity of the critic matters far less, because the criticism must persuade on its merits. When the report decides, in secret, whether anyone will read the paper at all, the absence of a name becomes the absence of accountability.

Some journals have begun to experiment along these lines, publishing papers first and attaching the referees' reports afterwards, sometimes signed and sometimes not. Such arrangements are not free of costs: they may let weak work into circulation, and they ask readers to do more of the sorting. But they reveal what the familiar arrangement conceals, namely that the case for anonymous review has always rested on an assumption about what review is for. If review exists to improve papers, a mask does little harm. If it exists to decide which papers exist, the mask deserves far more suspicion than it usually receives.

  3. (C)junior researchers need the protection of anonymity, since senior figures in their fields often control decisions about their promotion.
  4. (D)anonymous review should be abolished outright, because referees routinely use their concealment to suppress the work of their rivals.

Paragraph 3 states the thesis: anonymity is not itself the problem; 'What makes it dangerous is its combination with gatekeeping power.' Paragraph 4 restates it: if review only improves papers 'a mask does little harm', but if it decides 'which papers exist' it deserves suspicion.

CAT-VARC-P06-050 · Science as an institution · passage Medium · 443 words · 4 questions

Discussions of the so-called file drawer problem usually begin with journals. Editors, the complaint runs, prefer results that show something: a drug that works, an effect that appears, a difference between groups. Studies that find nothing are harder to place, and so they remain in researchers' filing cabinets while the published literature fills up with positive findings. A reader who surveys that literature will then overestimate how often, and how strongly, the effect in question occurs, since the failures that would have tempered the estimate never reached print. The literature, in other words, is not a sample of the research that was done but a selection from it, made on the very quantity that readers most want to estimate.

There is truth in this account, and the remedy it suggests, journals that commit in advance to publishing well-conducted studies whatever they find, deserves the support it has received. But the account places the drawer in the wrong office. Ask a working scientist what became of her last inconclusive experiment and the answer is seldom that an editor turned it down. More often it was never written up at all. The study was abandoned before any journal had a chance to reject it, and it sits in the drawer because its own author put it there.

The reason is not laziness. A positive result, whatever its other defects, announces itself: something happened. A null result is mute about its own meaning. It may show that the effect does not exist, or that the sample was too small to detect it, or that a reagent was contaminated, or that a student misread the protocol. The researcher who obtained it is often the person least able to tell which, and the most honest description she could offer, that she looked and did not see, is one that few colleagues would think worth the trouble of reading. Writing such a paper costs weeks and earns little, and the decision to move on is, from where she stands, entirely rational.

If this is right, obliging journals to accept null results treats the symptom at the far end. The drawer will empty only when null results become informative, and that depends on decisions made before any data are collected: samples large enough that a real effect would probably have been seen, procedures specified in advance so that a failure cannot be blamed afterwards on improvisation, checks built in to show that the apparatus was working. A null result obtained under such conditions says something definite, and researchers will publish it for the same reason they publish anything, because it tells their colleagues something they did not know.

CAT-VARC-P06-050-Q1 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ
According to the passage, a published literature from which failed studies are missing leads its readers to
  1. (A)treat the positive studies they read as if those studies had been conducted with unusually large samples.
  2. (B)overestimate both how frequently the effect being studied occurs and how strong it tends to be when it does.
  3. (C)doubt the positive findings they read, because they suspect that many failed studies have been quietly hidden from them.
  4. (D)assume that journals reject every study that fails to find a difference between the groups it compares.

Paragraph 1 says the reader 'will then overestimate how often, and how strongly, the effect in question occurs, since the failures that would have tempered the estimate never reached print.'

Common misconception: Choosing the option about readers' suspicion, which reverses the effect described.

CAT-VARC-P06-051 · Science as an institution · passage Easy · 433 words · 4 questions

When professional scientists first began to rely on volunteers to record birds, butterflies or the timing of spring flowers, the objection most often raised was that amateurs would make mistakes. A trained ornithologist can tell two similar warblers apart by a fragment of song; a weekend walker may not know that there are two species to distinguish. Such worries were not idle, for a single confident error repeated across many records can manufacture a trend that does not exist. Data gathered by such walkers, the argument went, would be riddled with misidentifications, and conclusions drawn from it would be built on sand.

The worry was reasonable, but experience has shown it to be aimed at the lesser target. Well-run projects have learned to ask volunteers for the kinds of observation they can make reliably, such as whether any frog was heard calling at a pond on a given evening, rather than the kinds that demand years of training. Where identification is genuinely hard, volunteers can photograph what they see and leave the naming to specialists, and records that look improbable can be flagged and checked. Errors do not vanish, but when thousands of independent observations are pooled, isolated mistakes tend to be swamped by the many correct reports around them.

The more stubborn problem lies not in what volunteers see but in where and when they look. People record wildlife near their homes, along popular footpaths and on fine weekends, so that their records cluster wherever leisure, access and good weather happen to coincide. They visit nature reserves more often than industrial estates, and gardens more often than farmland. A map of volunteer sightings is therefore partly a map of the species and partly a map of the observers, and no amount of skill in identification will separate the two. A bird that seems to be thriving in the suburbs may simply be living where the watchers live.

This bias is harder to correct than misidentification precisely because it is invisible in any single record. Each observation may be impeccable; the distortion appears only in the pattern. Projects that take it seriously ask some volunteers to survey assigned locations at fixed times, accepting fewer and less convenient records in exchange for a sample whose shape is known. Such schemes demand more of participants, and they rarely attract the crowds that open recording does. But they show that the reliability of citizen science was never really a question about the competence of amateurs. It is a question about whether the people designing a project have thought as carefully about sampling as about skill.

  3. (C)explain why well-run projects began asking volunteers to photograph what they had observed.
  4. (D)prove that volunteer records of birds have turned out to be less accurate than records of flowers.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 1 reports 'the objection most often raised' that amateurs would err; the warbler example shows the kind of misidentification feared: the expert distinguishes two species that the walker 'may not know' exist.

Why not (B): The passage makes no comparison between song and appearance; the fragment of song only shows what the trained expert can do.

Why not (C): Photography is mentioned in paragraph 2 as a later response to hard identification; the warbler example belongs to the critics' objection in paragraph 1.

Why not (D): Accuracy is never compared across groups of organisms, and the claim of proof is far stronger than anything in paragraph 1.

Common misconception: Linking the example to the photographing practice of paragraph 2 rather than to the objection it sits in.

Difficulty: The function is clear from the sentences before and after the example. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P06-052 · Science as an institution · passage Medium · 441 words · 4 questions

For much of the last century, efforts to explain science to the public rested on a simple picture. People who resisted a scientific conclusion, it was assumed, did so because they did not understand it; the gap between expert and lay opinion was a gap in knowledge, and it could be closed by supplying the missing facts. Critics later gave this picture a name, the deficit model, and the name stuck because it captured what was wrong with it: it treated the public as a vessel with a hole in it, defined entirely by what it lacked.

The model fails first on its own terms. If resistance came from ignorance, those who knew most about a contested subject would be the most accepting of the expert view, yet it is easy to find people who have read widely on a topic and still reject the consensus, sometimes with more conviction than before they began. Knowledge, it turns out, can be recruited to defend a position as readily as to change it. A person who already distrusts the source of a claim does not experience new information as enlightenment; she experiences it as further pressure from that source. The facts are heard, but they are heard as moves in a contest whose sides were chosen before the conversation began.

The deeper failure concerns what the model leaves out. Consider a village told by a team of engineers that a new dam upstream poses no danger. The villagers may follow the hydrology perfectly well. What they also know, and what the engineers' presentation omits, is that the last assurance they received from officials turned out to be wrong, that the benefits of the dam will flow to a distant city, and that if anything goes wrong it is they, not the engineers, who will bear the loss. Their scepticism is not a vacancy waiting to be filled. It is a judgement about whom to trust and who pays, and it may be quite rational even when the hydrology is sound.

None of this means that facts are irrelevant or that every misunderstanding is secretly a stance about power. Some people do hold mistaken beliefs simply because nobody has explained a matter clearly, and for them a good explanation is exactly what is needed. The error of the deficit model was to make this the whole story. Communication that begins by asking what an audience already knows, fears and has experienced will sometimes end in explanation. But it will at least explain the right thing, and it will treat the people it addresses as parties to a decision rather than as containers for information.

  3. (C)regarded the press as distorting expert opinion on its way from the laboratory to ordinary readers.
  4. (D)regarded ordinary people as defined only by an absence of knowledge that experts were expected to fill.

Paragraph 1 says the model assumed resistance came from not understanding, to be fixed by 'supplying the missing facts', and it 'treated the public as a vessel with a hole in it, defined entirely by what it lacked'. The image means the public was seen only as a lack to be filled.

Why not (B): This reads the hole as a leak; the same sentence glosses the image as being 'defined entirely by what it lacked', which is about emptiness, not about failing to retain.

Why not (C): The press is never mentioned; the image concerns the supposed emptiness of the public mind.

Common misconception: Taking 'hole' to mean a leak and so choosing the option about retaining facts.

Difficulty: The image is glossed in the same sentence. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET
