CAT-VARC-P06-053 · Science as an institution · passage Medium · 461 words · 4 questions

Generations of students have learned that science proceeds by a method, and that the method has steps. One observes, forms a hypothesis, designs an experiment, tests the hypothesis and draws a conclusion, after which the cycle begins again. The sequence is tidy, easy to examine and, as a description of what scientists actually do, very nearly useless. It is not that no scientist has ever followed it. It is that the sciences differ so widely in what they are able to do that any single recipe either excludes much of their work or becomes so general that it no longer guides anyone.

Consider the range. An astronomer studying a distant star cannot intervene in it; she can only watch, and her ingenuity lies in finding natural cases that differ in just the respect she wants to compare. A geologist reconstructing how a mountain range formed is not testing a hypothesis by experiment but assembling a history from traces, much as a detective does, and her standard of success is the coherence of a narrative that accounts for scattered evidence. A laboratory chemist may indeed run controlled experiments, but she spends much of her time on something the textbook sequence ignores entirely: making instruments and procedures work at all. To call all of this 'the scientific method' is to give one name to several distinct crafts.

The tidy sequence is not merely inaccurate; it does some damage. By making the controlled experiment the model of all good science, it encourages the view that sciences which cannot experiment are somehow lesser, as though reconstructing the history of the Earth were a provisional activity awaiting a proper laboratory. And by presenting science as a machine that turns hypotheses into conclusions, it leaves the public poorly prepared for the ordinary untidiness of research, in which results conflict, estimates are revised and experts disagree for long periods before they converge. People taught to expect the machine are apt to read each revision as a malfunction.

Is there, then, nothing that the sciences share? There is something, but it is not a procedure. What unites the astronomer, the geologist and the chemist is a set of habits about how claims are treated once they have been made: they are exposed to colleagues who are expected to attack them, they are stated in a form that evidence could tell against, and the people who make them are expected to give them up when such evidence arrives. These are norms of a community rather than steps for an individual, and that is precisely why they cannot be captured in a flowchart. A student who had absorbed them would understand science better than one who had memorised the steps, even if she could not say in what order they come.

  3. (C)her account of past events hangs together and explains the scattered evidence she has found.
  4. (D)the instruments she has built for the purpose can be made to work reliably in the field and laboratory.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 2 says the geologist assembles 'a history from traces' and that 'her standard of success is the coherence of a narrative that accounts for scattered evidence'.

Why not (A): Paragraph 2 says the geologist 'is not testing a hypothesis by experiment' at all, so this cannot be her standard.

Why not (B): This is the ingenuity paragraph 2 attributes to the astronomer, who 'cannot intervene' and must find suitable natural cases.

Why not (D): Making instruments work is what paragraph 2 says occupies the chemist; it is not given as the geologist's standard.

Common misconception: Confusing the three scientists of paragraph 2 with one another.

CAT-VARC-P06-054 · Science as an institution · passage Medium · 436 words · 4 questions

When two researchers, one celebrated and one unknown, publish a discovery together, the discovery is very likely to be remembered as the celebrated researcher's. The same pattern appears when two people arrive at a result independently: the one who is already eminent tends to receive the larger share of the attention, the citations and the invitations that follow. Sociologists of science named this the Matthew effect, after the verse in the Gospel of Matthew about those who have being given more, and it is usually discussed as a problem of fairness. The unknown co-author did much of the work, and someone else collected the credit.

That description is correct within its limits, but it understates what is at stake, and it also misses why the effect is so persistent. Scientists face a literature far larger than anyone could read in a lifetime. A familiar name on a paper is a cheap signal that the paper is probably worth the reader's time, and in a world of scarce attention, readers who rely on such signals are not being foolish. To some degree, then, the effect is the by-product of a reasonable strategy for coping with abundance. Condemning it as mere snobbery does not explain why careful and fair-minded people take part in it every day.

The trouble begins when attention is converted into resources. A researcher whose papers are widely read finds it easier to win grants, which pay for students and equipment, which produce more papers, which are read more widely still. Advantage compounds. After a few rounds, the difference between two careers may bear little relation to any difference in the quality of their first ideas, and the field as a whole has placed a heavy bet on one line of work for reasons that are partly accidental. Ideas that happened to originate with the less visible researcher may wait years for a hearing, or never receive one, not because they were tested and found wanting but because nobody with the means to test them was looking in their direction.

Seen in this light, the effect is less an injustice done to individuals than a distortion in how a field allocates its bets, and the remedy is not to forbid readers from noticing names, which would be both futile and costly. It is to loosen the link between attention and money: to judge some grant proposals without knowing who wrote them, to set aside funds for researchers early in their careers, and to treat a famous name as a reason to read a paper rather than as a reason to believe it.

CAT-VARC-P06-054-Q1 · Inference · EasyMCQ
The passage suggests that readers who give priority to papers by well-known authors are
  1. (A)responding sensibly to the impossibility of reading everything that is published in their field.
  2. (B)unaware that the unknown co-authors of such papers usually did most of the work that the papers report.
  3. (C)behaving unfairly and ought to be trained to ignore the names that are attached to the research they read.
  4. (D)motivated mainly by admiration for eminent figures rather than by any practical consideration of their own.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 2 says the literature is 'far larger than anyone could read', that a familiar name is 'a cheap signal' of worth, and that readers who rely on it 'are not being foolish'.

Why not (B): Paragraph 1 says the unknown co-author 'did much of the work' in one illustrative case; readers' awareness is never discussed, and 'usually' generalises the example.

Why not (C): Paragraph 4 says forbidding readers to notice names 'would be both futile and costly', so the author would not recommend training them to ignore names.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 rejects 'Condemning it as mere snobbery' and explains the habit as 'a reasonable strategy for coping with abundance', which is a practical consideration.

CAT-VARC-P06-054-Q2 · Author's tone / attitude · MediumMCQ
The author's attitude toward the view that the Matthew effect is 'a problem of fairness' is best described as
  1. (A)treating it with open contempt as a complaint voiced only by those who have themselves been denied credit.
  2. (B)rejecting it entirely, on the ground that credit in science is in fact distributed according to merit.

Answer: (C)

CAT-VARC-P06-054-Q3 · Application / analogy · MediumMCQ
Which of the following measures is most in keeping with the remedy the author proposes?
  1. (A)A university that awards prizes to junior co-authors whose contributions to celebrated papers went unrecognised.
  2. (B)A journal that prints every paper without the names of its authors, so that readers cannot be swayed by reputation.
  3. (C)A professional society that publishes a yearly ranking of the most frequently cited researchers in each field.
  4. (D)A funding agency that assesses a share of the applications it receives with the applicants' names and records removed.

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 4 says the remedy is 'to loosen the link between attention and money', for example 'to judge some grant proposals without knowing who wrote them'. Blind assessment of some applications does this.

Why not (A): This addresses credit owed to individuals, which paragraph 4 calls the lesser issue; it does nothing to loosen the link between attention and money.

Why not (B): Paragraph 4 says the remedy 'is not to forbid readers from noticing names, which would be both futile and costly'; hiding authors from readers is exactly that.

Why not (C): A citation ranking advertises the attention already received and would strengthen, not loosen, the conversion of attention into resources.

CAT-VARC-P06-054-Q4 · Inference · DifficultMCQ
It can be inferred that, in the author's view, the most serious consequence of the Matthew effect is that
  1. (A)unknown researchers leave science altogether because they receive too little credit for the work they have done.
  2. (B)a field may commit its resources to some ideas and neglect others for reasons that have little to do with their merit.
  3. (C)well-known researchers gradually come to believe that their own ideas are better than those of their colleagues.
  4. (D)readers stop judging papers for themselves and simply accept whatever a famous author happens to have written.

Paragraph 4 states that the effect is 'less an injustice done to individuals than a distortion in how a field allocates its bets'.

Why not (A): Departure from science is not mentioned; the passage describes ideas waiting 'years for a hearing', not people leaving the profession.

CAT-VARC-P06-055 · Science as an institution · passage Difficult · 598 words · 5 questions

Every year, research funding agencies ask panels of scientists to read hundreds of proposals and rank them, and every year the money runs out long before the list does. The proposals near the bottom are usually easy to set aside: they are poorly argued, or unoriginal, or plainly unworkable. The difficulty lies higher up. Among the proposals that every member of the panel agrees are good, the panel must still decide which will be funded and which will not, and the line it draws often separates projects that no one present could distinguish with any confidence. It is in response to this predicament that a few agencies have begun to experiment with a once unthinkable idea: setting a threshold of quality and then choosing among the proposals above it by lot.

The proposal is usually defended on grounds of efficiency. Ranking is laborious; panels spend weeks producing distinctions that a lottery would produce in minutes. This is true, but it is the weakest part of the case, and resting on it invites the reply that good decisions are worth what they cost. The stronger argument is about honesty. Experience suggests that two independent panels given the same proposals will often disagree about which of them fall just above the funding line and which just below, a sign that, near the top, the ranking reflects the composition of the panel at least as much as the merits of the work. A ranking of this kind already contains a large element of chance. What it lacks is any acknowledgement of the fact, and so its outcomes are experienced, by winners and losers alike, as verdicts on quality.

Hidden chance is not neutral chance. When the members of a panel cannot tell good proposals apart on their merits, other preferences fill the gap: a taste for methods they themselves use, a wariness of projects whose payoff is uncertain, a familiarity with applicants they have met. Each of these is a mild bias, and none need be deliberate, but together they tilt the funding line toward the safe and the recognisable. A lottery among the proposals above the threshold removes this tilt at the margin. It cannot make the panel bolder, but it can stop the panel's caution from deciding cases that its judgement cannot decide.

The most serious objection is not that lotteries abandon judgement, since judgement is exercised in full at the threshold, where panels are reasonably good at separating the sound from the unsound. It is that scientists need to believe that reward follows merit, and that a lottery, by announcing that it sometimes does not, will corrode the motivation on which research depends. This deserves to be taken seriously. Yet the belief it seeks to protect is, at the margin, already false, and a system that preserves morale by concealing how it works purchases that morale at the cost of misleading the very people it rewards. A researcher whose proposal loses a draw has at least been told the truth: that her work was judged fundable and that the money ran out.

None of this recommends lotteries everywhere. Where proposals differ in ways that can be measured, as when an agency must choose a single design for a costly instrument, ranking is the right tool and chance would be an abdication. The argument applies only where judgement has reached its limit. Its real lesson concerns not lotteries but the pretence of precision, and agencies that decline to draw lots might still learn from it, for instance by publishing how close the decisions near their funding line actually were.

CAT-VARC-P06-055-Q1 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ
According to the passage, panels judging research proposals are reasonably good at
  1. (A)separating proposals that are scientifically sound from those that are not, at the threshold of quality.
  2. (B)ranking the strongest proposals in the order of their likely scientific value to the field.
  3. (C)recognising when their own preferences have influenced a decision about where the funding line falls.
  4. (D)predicting which of the riskier projects before them will eventually produce the largest payoff.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 4 says judgement 'is exercised in full at the threshold, where panels are reasonably good at separating the sound from the unsound'.

Why not (B): Paragraphs 1 and 2 say the opposite: near the top, the line 'often separates projects that no one present could distinguish with any confidence'.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 says the biases at the margin need not be 'deliberate', which suggests panels may not notice them, not that they recognise them.

Why not (D): Paragraph 3 describes panels as wary of 'projects whose payoff is uncertain'; nothing says they predict payoffs well.

Common misconception: Assuming that panels which judge soundness well must also rank well, which paragraphs 1 and 2 deny.

Difficulty: The answer is stated in one clause of paragraph 4. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P06-055-Q2 · Structure / function · MediumMCQ

CAT-VARC-P06-055-Q3 · Inference · MediumMCQ
The sentence 'Hidden chance is not neutral chance' (paragraph 3) implies that

  2. (B)lotteries are themselves biased, because the proposals entered into them are chosen by cautious panels.

CAT-VARC-P06-055-Q4 · Strengthen / weaken / assumption · DifficultMCQ

  1. (A)Some researchers say they would rather lose a draw than be told that their proposal was ranked just below the funding line.
  2. (B)Projects ranked just above the funding line have consistently gone on to outperform those ranked just below it.
  3. (C)Agencies that fund by lottery spend considerably less on assessing proposals than agencies that rank every proposal they receive.
  4. (D)Members of a panel sometimes disagree about whether a borderline proposal is sound enough to be entered into the draw.

Answer: (B)

The reply in paragraph 4 rests on the premise that the belief that reward follows merit 'is, at the margin, already false'. Why not (A): This supports the reply, which holds that the loser of a draw 'has at least been told the truth'; a preference for that outcome strengthens rather than weakens it.

Why not (C): This concerns the efficiency argument of paragraph 2, which the author calls the weakest part of the case; it does not touch the reply about motivation.

Why not (D): This bears on the claim that panels judge well at the threshold, which answers a different objection in paragraph 4; occasional disagreement is also compatible with being 'reasonably good'.

CAT-VARC-P06-055-Q5 · Main idea / primary purpose · DifficultMCQ
Which of the following best captures the central argument of the passage?
  1. (A)Agencies should publish how close their decisions near the funding line were instead of adopting lotteries at all.
  2. (B)Where panels cannot reliably separate good proposals, drawing lots is preferable to a ranking that disguises chance as judgement.

Why not (A): Paragraph 5 offers publication as something agencies 'that decline to draw lots might still learn'; the author does not recommend it in place of lotteries, which the passage defends where 'judgement has reached its limit'.

Why not (C): Paragraph 2 calls efficiency 'the weakest part of the case'; the author's central argument is about honesty.

Why not (D): Paragraph 4 says panels judge well 'at the threshold', and paragraph 5 says lotteries are not recommended 'everywhere', so both the call for wholesale replacement and the claim of bias at every stage overstate the case.

CAT-VARC-P06-056 · Science as an institution · passage Difficult · 586 words · 5 questions

A scientific paper presents itself as a complete set of instructions. Its methods section lists the materials, the quantities, the temperatures and the timings, and the implicit promise is that anyone competent who follows them will see what the authors saw. This promise underwrites one of the most cherished ideas about science: that its knowledge is public, available in principle to anyone who can read, and independent of the particular people who first produced it. When a laboratory fails to reproduce a published result, the natural suspicion therefore falls either on the original claim or on the care of the people who tried to repeat it.

Anyone who has worked at a laboratory bench knows that the promise is only partly kept. Many techniques depend on skills that their practitioners cannot fully articulate: how firmly to tap a tube, how long a mixture should look cloudy before it is ready, which faint smudge on an image is signal and which is dirt. Such knowledge is not secret, and it is not mystical. It is simply of a kind that is acquired by doing and by watching others do, in the way that one learns to ride a bicycle rather than the way one learns a telephone number. A methods section can point toward it, but it cannot transmit it, any more than a manual can make its reader a competent cyclist.

It is not unusual for a group trying to build a new kind of instrument from published descriptions alone to fail again and again, while another group that has sent a member to spend some weeks in the original laboratory, or has received a visitor from it, succeeds, often without being able to say afterwards what the crucial difference was. In such cases the written instructions were not wrong. They were incomplete in ways that neither their authors nor their readers could have specified in advance, because the authors did not know which of the many things they did without thinking were the things that mattered.

Recognising this does not license complacency about how methods are reported. A great deal that is now left out of papers could be written down, and the habit of omitting it, whether to save space or to protect an advantage, deserves the criticism it receives. But the recognition does alter what we should expect from reform. Demanding ever longer methods sections will capture the explicit portion of a technique more fully while leaving the tacit portion exactly where it was. If the goal is that results can be checked by people other than those who produced them, some of the effort now spent on documents might be better spent on movement: on funding researchers to visit one another's laboratories, on recording procedures as they are actually performed, and on treating the training of a replicator as part of the cost of a replication.

There is a larger consequence as well. The picture of scientific knowledge as a body of public statements, detachable from the people who hold it, turns out to be an idealisation. Much of what a field knows lives in the hands of its practitioners and moves only when they do. This is not a reason to distrust that knowledge, since skills can be tested by their results as surely as statements can. It is a reason to treat the communities that carry it, and the slow work of passing it on, as part of the infrastructure of science rather than as a private matter of training.

CAT-VARC-P06-056-Q1 · Meaning in context · MediumMCQ
In paragraph 2, 'the promise' that 'is only partly kept' is the idea that
  1. (A)a competent reader who follows a paper's written methods will be able to obtain the findings it reports.
  2. (B)researchers will share their materials and equipment with any laboratory that asks to repeat their work.
  3. (C)scientific knowledge will in time be cleared of the errors made by the people who first produced it.
  4. (D)a failed replication shows that either the original claim or the replicators' own care must be at fault.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 1 says 'the implicit promise is that anyone competent who follows them will see what the authors saw', and paragraph 2 says this promise 'is only partly kept' because tacit skills cannot be written down.

Why not (B): Sharing materials is never mentioned; the promise in paragraph 1 is that anyone 'who follows' the listed methods 'will see what the authors saw'.

Why not (C): Paragraph 1 describes knowledge as 'independent of the particular people who first produced it', which concerns independence from persons, not the correction of their errors.

Why not (D): This is the 'natural suspicion' that paragraph 1 says follows from the promise; it is a consequence of believing the promise, not the promise itself.

Common misconception: Choosing the 'natural suspicion', which is closely tied to the promise but is what follows from it.

Difficulty: The referent lies in the previous paragraph, and one wrong option is the consequence stated two sentences after the promise. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P06-056-Q2 · Inference · MediumMCQ
It can be inferred from paragraph 3 that the groups that succeeded after a visit
  1. (A)were given secret details that the original laboratory had deliberately kept out of its published papers.
  2. (B)had discovered errors in the published descriptions, which the original authors then went on to correct.
  3. (C)may have acquired the decisive skill without identifying which part of the procedure it concerned.
  4. (D)were more careful and more competent than the groups that had worked only from the published papers.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 3 says the successful groups often could not 'say afterwards what the crucial difference was', because even the original authors 'did not know which of the many things they did without thinking were the things that mattered'. The skill was transferred without being identified.

Why not (A): Paragraph 2 says such knowledge 'is not secret', and paragraph 3 says the authors themselves 'did not know' which of their actions mattered, so nothing was deliberately withheld.

Why not (B): Paragraph 3 says 'the written instructions were not wrong', only incomplete, so there were no errors to discover.

Why not (D): The passage attributes success to contact with the original laboratory, not to greater care; paragraph 2 says the missing knowledge is 'acquired by doing and by watching others do'.

CAT-VARC-P06-056-Q3 · Application / analogy · DifficultMCQ
Which of the following situations is most closely analogous to the account of instrument-building in paragraph 3?
  1. (A)A baker refuses to reveal one key ingredient, and rivals who copy her bread cannot match its flavour until a former employee discloses what it is.
  2. (B)Cooks who follow a recipe all succeed at the first attempt, but only those with many years of experience are able to explain why each of its steps matters.
  3. (C)A recipe is printed with a wrong oven temperature, and the cooks who follow it keep failing until the publisher prints a correction in the next edition.
  4. (D)Cooks using a baker's detailed recipe keep failing to match her bread until one spends a week in her kitchen, after which he succeeds without knowing what he changed.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 3 describes groups that fail from written descriptions alone and succeed after time in the original laboratory, 'often without being able to say afterwards what the crucial difference was', while the instructions themselves 'were not wrong'. Only the baker's kitchen case has all three features: accurate but insufficient instructions, success through presence, and an unidentified difference.

Why not (A): This depends on deliberate secrecy, whereas paragraph 2 says the knowledge 'is not secret' and paragraph 3 says even the authors did not know what mattered.

Why not (B): In paragraph 3 the readers of the instructions fail; here they succeed, so the situation reverses the pattern the passage describes.

Why not (C): Here the instructions are wrong and a written correction fixes them; paragraph 3 says 'the written instructions were not wrong', and the missing element could not be written down.

Common misconception: Choosing the secret-ingredient case because a visit or an insider also brings success there, overlooking that the passage rules out secrecy.

Difficulty: Each wrong option matches part of the pattern; the correct answer must match failure from accurate text, success through presence, and an unarticulated difference all at once. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P06-056-Q4 · Inference · DifficultMCQ
The author would most likely agree with which of the following?

  2. (B)Knowledge carried in the skills of practitioners is less reliable than knowledge that has been stated explicitly in published papers.
  3. (C)A field could report its techniques far more fully than it now does and still find that some of its results are hard to reproduce elsewhere.

Why not (D): Paragraph 4 says 'A great deal that is now left out of papers could be written down' and that omitting it 'deserves the criticism it receives', so reporting reform is not wasted.

CAT-VARC-P06-056-Q5 · Except / not supported · DifficultMCQ
Each of the following is proposed or endorsed by the author as a way of helping others check published results EXCEPT
  1. (A)paying for researchers to spend time working in one another's laboratories.
  2. (B)counting the training of those who attempt a replication as part of what the replication costs.
  3. (C)including in papers the details that are now omitted to save space or to keep an advantage.
  4. (D)lengthening methods sections until each of them captures the whole of the technique it describes.

Answer: (D)

Why not (A): Paragraph 4 proposes 'funding researchers to visit one another's laboratories', so this is endorsed.

Why not (B): Paragraph 4 proposes 'treating the training of a replicator as part of the cost of a replication', so this is endorsed.

Why not (C): Paragraph 4 says much that is left out 'could be written down' and that omitting it 'whether to save space or to protect an advantage, deserves the criticism it receives', so the author endorses including it.
